JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

THAT PRIMROSE MYSTERY. May I, by a recollection of my own, supplement the editor's note respecting Primrose Day ? The first wreath of primroses which Queen Victoria sent to be laid upon the grave of Lord Beaconsfield was inscribed: " His favourite flower." Who was lie? The dead statesman? So it was gererally supposed, and the verbal inadequacy and vagueness decidedly lent colour to the rea.onable enough assumption. But there is an entire absence of evidence that Lord Beacons- field had an especial preference for the primrose, whereas it was well known to bare been the favontite flower of the late Puke() Consot t. whose premature death the bereaved Queen never ceased to mourn. I think that one who carefully considers all the circum- stances and is not blirdfd by prejudice can hardly doubt that, alter all, her Majesty had the late Prince Albert in mind when she penned the sentence in question. The opportunity afforded by the indefinite wording, however, was much too good to be lost, and we all mow how suece!•sfully party wire-pullers hare since exploited the primrose MOTOR SPEEDS.

At the Stockton Heath Parish Conneil's annual meeting, a good &A of isdignation was expreoted on account of the reticles 4 scorching of road hogs in charge of motor cars I was reminded of an oceasi, n not more than half-a-dozen years ago, when certain Lancashire authofities song' t to include in their by-lass a regulation forbidding motorists to drive at a speed exceeding the rate of ten miles an hour while passing through their borough. Among the residents was a wealthy mf mber of the Unif xi, and he brcught in opposition to the proposal a formidable array of counsel and witnesses booked by the interested organisation, and so contrived to overwhelm the pro- moters' case et the Government inquiry. I spoke to the local police superintendf nt on the subj,ct, and he did not at all regret the result He said the existing law was ample if properly admintsts red ; indeed, persenally, he would rather not be hempered by the imposition of a speed limit. In taXing proceedings under the Statute, the prosecution had simply to prove that an alleged ( ffender wrs driving to the danger of the public; in the other case it wou'd cf course be incumbent on one t , show that dtfendant had actually exceeded specified seed. TAR-SPRAYED ROADS.

The Grappenhall Parish Cotacil are urging the County Council to tar-spray the main roads within their area, an undertaking so to do being as yet unfulfilled. I remember that a w hile ego the question of the dangerous state of the roads engaged the attention of the Lancashire Farmers' Association at a niretinct at Preston, and what occurred thete wruld interest tho:e in our own distrtct who are agitating for more and more tar-spraying. The Fecretary reported to the meeting at Preston now referred to that be had written to the Lancashire County Council surveyor, and had received a reply that the County Council were placed in a difficult position. On the one hand they had complaints from farmers as to th 3 dust nuisance if tar-spraying had not been done, and again they received complaints as to the slippery nature of the road as the result of tar-soraying. In the course of the ensuing discussion. Mr. P e (GooFnargh) stated that tar-sprayed roads were very touch appreciated by farmers generally, but Mr. Whittaker (Wipan) pointed out that the reads near Wigan were like glass through the tar-spraying.

The contributor to the discussion now introduced states that in the summer, when cycling about the country, he frequently noticed cases of isolated bees crawling in a dazed fashion no the tarred roads. "I noticed thM so frequently" he goes on, "that, out of curiosity, I often got off my machine to, watch them more closely. I concluded that they wore overcome by the heat. I should have thought no Mcre about the matter had I not been attracted by the peculiar antics of one or tao of them. Again I carefully observed them, and had to come to the con- clusion that they were intoxicated. Looking round and about I discovered two of them and a wasp, euckirg at a broken tar-blister in the road. I v.aited. In a little while they crawled at d lurched away, decidedly drunk or gassed ; more likely the latter. Some months after this I read of the plague amongst the bees in the Isle of Wight and other places. But I did not then connect this with what I bad observed ; it was not till some weeks after that I did so." EXTRAORDINARY PRECAIITIOUS.

'I he peculiar compensation case at the Warrington County Court last week reminded me of an experience which a relative of my own had some years ago. He was wool-buyer for a big Yorkshire firm, and returning from the London sales had the misfortune to suffer serious injury through a railway collision. When he was able to get about again, it has found that his eyesight, was permanently impaired and that he would no longer be able to follow his lucrative employment. Upon this he based a claim for substantial compensation. It was resited, and action was accordingly taken to recover the amount through the courts. While the suit was pending, the lawyers on the other side sent to him experts with samples of wool to test the truth sof Ws allegations as to permanent disablement. They pretended to be merchants who, having beard of his skill and know- ledge, were anxious for his judgment upon the specimens submitted. .ts it happened, I e was quite unable to oblige them. Subsolnentlf the attempt to catch him with guile was expcsed, and he obtained the full amount of his claim. LATEST MEMORIAL TABLET.

Al ng s ith many thcrs, I was intensely interested in the report of last week's public proceedings in Howard Pince, and of the speeches at the meeting in the Old Academy which followed the unveiling cere- mony. Without any thought of comparison between subjects so sharply contrasted at all points, I venture to suggest that tfe memory of Mrs. Barbauld is to this district what that of the Brontes is to Haworth. As to the formalities of Thursday week, how thcroughly justified was the resolution of the promoters to await the convenience of Mrs. Ridgway as to the (bite of the function, and how happy was the choice of other speakers en the important occasion ! MRS. BARBAULD.

As cne of this gifted lady's sincere admirers, I must needs add a few remarks to wl at has already been said and written to the subject in and thr, ugh these columns. Was it not a volume of miscellaneous poems, and not merely one pcem as observed by Mr, John Fairhurst— certainly, on the whole a more reliable authority than I can pretend to be—for which four editions were called in one Tear? After Mr. and Mrs. Barbauld'a experiences at Palgrave, where the latter helped the former in his school, the pair retired to Hampstead ; and it was there that "the virago," as ono irate and extremely rude partisan of the oppc site persuasion unwarrantibly termed Mrs Bdrbauld, wrote many political tracts in the Whig interest—l am free to at least touch on this in passing, though of course those prominently engaged in the unveiling of the tablet were precluded from mention- ing such matters. The stanza on life with which Mrs Ridgway close d her excellent address was much admired by Rogers as well as by Wordsworth. In concluding his speech to the vote of thanks, Alderman Bennett evidently had in mind Mrs. Barbauld'a "Hymn to content." Her " Ode to Spring," Ly the bye has been described as a happy imitation of Collins - to an edition of whose works she prefixed a characteristic critical essay. It will be remembered how she apostro- phises Sprirg as " Sweet daughter of a rough and stormy sire ; hoar Winter's blooming child," and a little later descries the season as " more sweet than softest touch of Done reed or Lydian flute," a typical verse running thus . .

- " Sweet is thy reign, but short ; the red dog star Stall scorch thy tresses ; and the mower's scythe Thy greens, thy flowerets all llemorselers hall destroy." I wonder how many thtusand odors to spring have been penned by minor poets since the foregoing was written? Anyhow, I imagine that but a very small percentage of the male section of these aspirants would have the courage to publish a candid "Ode to my tailor" or other tradesman.

LESSONS OF THE DISASTER.

BOATS FOR ALL PASSENGERS.

REMARKABLE EVIDENCE AT THE " TITANIC " INQUIRY.

As one outcome of the " Titanic" catastrophe, the leading English passenger lines have infcrmed the Board of Trade that they have decided to provide enough boat,' and rafts for all the passengers on their ships ; but, °vying to the difficulty of getting suitable boats, this cannot be done immediately in all cases The cf mpanies have taken this step of their on accord.

"ensational evidence was given on Tuesday before the Senate Investigation C, mmittee at Washington into the loss of the Titanic. Mr. Pittman, the third ottie r, stated that it the time a the collision the liner was steaming at the greatest speed she had made since leaving Scuthampton—abcut 21:1,- knots. WitneEs left the steamer in a boat which was carrying about 10 people, although its capacil would not have been overtaxed by carrying CO. Wen the liner sai.k be heard cries and moans from those in the water, and he told his men to row in that direction, but the passengers demurred and he yielded to their reque t and did rot go to the resaue of the dr ownirg per pie The witness cenfirmed a statement as to the light of a strange vessel being seen at the time of the disaster. Frederick Fleet, the look-out man, said i! he had been provided with glasses he c uld have seen the iceberg in time to give sulliciert warning to the bridge. The men had asked for glasses, but had been told there were mire for them. A calculation in m the lis's of rescued shows that there is no ease of a man surviving and his women- folk or children being lost. Forty-one per cent of the rescued were women and 59 per cent men and children ; but taking passengers alone, and leaving out the sailors who manned the boats, the percentage was a omen saved, CS; men and children, 35 Sixty-one per cent of all the rescued were first-class passengers, CO second-class, 24 third-class, and 22 crew.

It transpired on Wednesday that the Leyland liner Californian was only about eighteen miles distant from the 'I itanic when she fiundered, and had the captain known of the big vessels plight all those on board might base been tesceed. Unhappily, the Califoinian's wireless apparatus was not wot king and the Titanic's appeals for help wet e, tberef( re, never received, n,r were the distress signals observed. •

The Californian lay to all nighi on the night of the disaster with engines stopled owing t) an immense icefield havirg been s.ghted. No C3planation is forthcoming as to what rut the Viieless apraiatus out of order.

MEMORIAL SERVICES.

At most of the places cf e orship in Warrington and district on Surday spcci .1 services were held in mem( ry of those who lost their lives in the Titanic disaster. There were large congregations and an impressive feature of the services was the singing of the hymn " Nearer my God to Thee." which VlBB played I,y the herr ic orchestra of the" Titanic" as the vessel gradually sank into the deep. In the serimns touching references were made to the catastrophe and its lessons, and the bravery displayed by pas.,engr rs and crew was held up to admiration.

===== 0002601_19120427_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
Warrington Parish Church.

The service at the Parish Church on Sunday evening was of a deeply impressive character. As an anthem the choir sang the Russian contakion of ,he departed "Give rest,o Christ,to Thy servants with thy Saints" to the beautFul Kief melody, adapted by Sir Walter Parma. This tcuching hymn formed part of the great memorial service in St. Paul's Cathedral. It is snag without accompaniment and its rendering by the Parish Church choir was very devotional and effective. The Rector (Rev. Canon Willis) preached on the words "It is appointed unto men race to die, and after this the judg- ment." He reviewed the circumstances of the disaster and emphasised the need for greater precautions such as the adoption of a more southerly course during certoin seasons and the provision of more lifeboats. He expressed regret that, according to the newspaper reports, there was little in the way of Sunc'ay observance on the" Titanic." It was apparently not deemed necessary to employ chaplains on the American liners because the journey was so short He appealed to the congregft Lion to resist the increasing encroach- ments of worldly pleasures upon the Christial observ- ance of the Lord's Day. With regard to the lessons of the text, we had nothing to fear, either from death cr from judgment if we euomitted our lives to our own judgment, repented, and so made ourselves fit for the call however sudden. After the blessing the hymn "Nearer, my God, to Thee" was sung and the crngre- gat ion then stood while Mr Smith who, in the absence of Mr Darby, was at the organ, played the Dead March in" Saul."

===== 0002601_19120427_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
St. Paul's Church.

A memorial service was hold at St. Paul's Church on Sunday evening. The Vicar (the Rev W Bracecarnp) preached from the text" And the sea gave up the dead which were in it." He said the disaster had happened to teach us many lessons, chief among them being that mon was entirely dependent upon God. It was pleasing to note that all on board, whether rich or poor, learned or unlearned, practically had the same chance. The man in the street, who was continually pointing the the finger of scorn at the millicnaires, could not say in this instance at any rate that because of their money they bad greater privileges than the rest of the passengers ; in fact, when they read the accounts, some of the millionaires showed great heroism and did all in their power to help others. Captain Smith's last command, after be had done his own duty, nobly, "Be British," would become as dear and familiar to us as the command of Nelson, "England expects every man this day to do do his duty." Surely the act cf the bandsmen, who stood midst all the danger, and could play that beautiful hymn "Nearer, my God, to Thee," could only be done in the power of the religion of their Lord Jesus.

The special hymns sung were, " Lead, kindly Light," " Days and moments quickly flying," and " Nearer, my God, to Thee." Mr, Ernest Wilkes presieed at the organ.

===== 0002601_19120427_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
St. Peter's Church.

There was a large congreg +don at a memorial service held at St. Peter's Church on Sunday evening. The service opened with the singing of the h3ruo " Eternal Father, strong to save" and the hymns Lead, kindly light," " Peace, perfect peace" and " Nearer, my God to thee," were also rendered. The serv7ce was con- ducted by the Vicar, the Rev B Percival, end tbe Rev H Garnett. In the course of his sermon, which he based on Psalm xlv 1, "God is cur refuge and strength, a very present help in trouble," the Vicar slid, the scut seeking assistance from God was similar to the action of the Titanic when it, sought the help of other ships, but the difference was that though the wireless telegrams reached their destination, those wh) received them were unable to respond, while on the other hand God was a very present help in time of trouble. God had made provision for the souls of men and note need perish, but, unfortunately, men put off taking advsn- tags of the safe provisions God had made for them. At the close of the service the o g inEt, Mr. E. A. Padmore, played the Dead March.

Emmanuel (Reformed) Church.

, - A inem)rial service was held on Sand ty evening. The choir rendered Stainer's anthem • What are these ?" and special hymns were suite, including "Eternal Father, strong to save," and "Nearer. my God, to Thee." At the close of the service the Dead March was played. The Rev W T Jaye, taking for his text, Job xiii. 15, " Though He slay me, yet will I trust in Him," said that with ninny there was a difficulty in believing in the goodness of God ; in fact, the inference was that, God was not good, or that He was impotent and be (the preacher) felt that many at the present time were asking—" How dot a God stand related to such ru appalling disaster as the loss of the Titanic with over 1,600 souls "? The Christian faith denied that pain toad affliction were evil. It seemed a hard thing to say to suffering men an 3 womeu that afflictions were good but. Jesna Christ, who was acquainted with grief said " Blessed are they that mourn," and the only way to solve the problem was to look at it from the Christian standpoint. Faith, if it did not wholly solve the problem of pain and affliction, at least suggested a way of solution, while unbelief had never removed a single difficulty, lie thanked God that the widow of the heroic commander of tie Titanic, in her deeply p thetic message of sympathy to her sorrowing sisters, brew bed the note of true faith in the closing words of Let let er— "May God Le with us and comfort us all." Just as Job bad the assurance that God, what- eve- He might permit, would be perfectly right, perfectly kind, so to-day under the shadow of the area' eat traritirre disaster in the world's history, men and women cold trust themselves to the mercy of God Emerson, in his evsay on "Heroism,' affirmed that tie courageous had not lived in vain " for every pursing eye is cheered by the vision." And all the world felt that the heroism displayed by men and women alike in what was an unprecedented catastrophe of the sea, would be talked abont by I,enerations yet unborn.

Wycliffe Congregational Church

At Wycliffe Congregational Church, the Rev. John Yonge preached from the text" When they shall say peace and safety, then sudden destruction cometh upon them. " Referring to incidents of the wreck the rev, gentleman continued : It is very much to 'be de.ubted whether any ship before has crossed the ocean with so many passengers belong- ing to the wealthy clams. It is said to have con- tained some twenty or thirty of the richest people in the world—millionaires and multi-millionaires. there were some of these moneyed classes who might be estimated as the even " vulgarly " rich, who apparently could spare a trifle of some £BOO to secure, for their own enjoyment, and that of their friends, for about six days, exclusive use of a n served promenade deck. The daily fare provided and the rates charged were even prodigious. There was one of these surprisingly wealthy men, who bad been to England and Europe upon his marriage tour, and he, with his bride, were home- ward bound. When the crash came, and the lifeboats were being tilled, the cry was—", Women first." This wealthy man begged to be allowed to accompany his bride. No doubt he would have paid handsomely for it. But the man in command of tho lifeboat was inexorable ; so the nuaband was left on the sinking ship, and the wife was taken in the lifeboat. Verily it was an awful separation. But es it proved, however, the scion of wealth and fame and position showed himself beyond everything a man, tor he was seen among the busiest in helping the women to share the rescue which was accorded to his wife. So in that way Colonel Astor

===== 0002601_19120427_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
LOST HIS LIFE, BUT. HIE SAVED. HIS MANHOOD,

and every true heart will feel for the young wife so awfully bereft of a hus')and •who proved himself so supremely to be a man. It is impossible not to give honour to the biave sea,' an, who could stand out against the frenzied appeal of the multi-millionaire, and refuse to give him place in the lifeboat, while there were helpless women yet to be saved. The sailor who refused, and the millionaire who yielded, are both entitled to a worthy fame, for such high principled and even heroio behaviour. Probably many, or even most of us, have scanned the list of survivors, without discovery of any personal friends. There was one name in the list of passengers, however, that most English people, at any rate, have eagerly looked for amJng the rescued, I n.ean

W. T. STEAD. He embarked on thit ship to mace a Chris'len humanitarian tour in America in aid of a new Christian crusade which is just being started. He had been engaged that Sunday night, in interesting conversation with fellow passengers upon many of the highest things. When the alarm was made, he at trice busied himself in seeking the rescue of others, and teak no part what- ever in seeking his own. Hence,the famous journalist, the apostle of purity, the one-time martyr and prisoner for his humanitarian zeal; the man known by kings and emperors, the apostle of a world's peace, las found unexpected burial beneath the octas! I had met him personally more than once; had followed his fine and heroic career more than 30 years with instinctive admiration, had held him in special honour as a Congreational Pastor's son—a scholar of Silcoate's school. He was a man of genius, courage, resource consistency, devotion, and paramount unselfishness. Ile has now passed, by utterly unpre- meditated pathway, through the di pths of ocean' to the right hand in the Heavens. How his death recalls the fine lines of Macaulay, in his Lays of Ancient Rome, which celebrates the heroism of IL talus ! Be risked his life for his country and for his fellow-men. And how cull man die better Than facing fearful odds For the ashes of his fathers, And the Temple of his gods? And how could William Stean die better, more fully in keeping with his career of supremo devotion to his fellows, than in his noble, heroic rescuing from terrible death at see, the helpless women passengers on that ship. His lite las been of continual and immense value to all sorts and conditions of men and women. He had always been busy with his pan, his speech, and his travels far and near in the cause of human welfare, and that death at sea is a fine, however sad, conclusion to it. Ile, being dead, 3et speaks in and by his death, and Stead's life and death will go n speaking for long years to come. May God sastain his widow and family by such reflections esthete, and may his memory never perish as a man who lived and died for men and women. I have not been able to say many things I could like to have said. But, in closing, may a e not fitly recall the marvellous closing scene which was ouacted, just as the curtain of the sea hid from view the sinking vessel. How brave and resolute were these musicians of the shir, who are mid to lava spent their latest breath in stratus of music, even amid the panic of dismay. And how absolutely fitting, and, infinitely pathetic, and tenderly soothing and helpful must have 10,.,en the strains of the band as they ceased, for ever, all other musio ard, with superb presence of mind and keenly accurate sense of the needo of bursting hearts on both the sinking ship and disappearing lifeboats, bearing asunder, for ever in this world, tendere,t and dearest ones, they ended their music and their lives, with the stains of the well-known melody,

. Nearer, my God, to Thee, Nearer to Thee! Even tho' it be a cross that raiseth me, Still all my song shall he, Nearer, my God, to The:,, Nearer to Thee ! I doubt, whether any such parting salutation or message or holy song was ever heard before as a wrecking ship tank beneath the ocean wave, and its strains were lifted by sinking, drowning can. They sounded, as military phrase would say, " The Laat Post." The path ios s complete and perfect. We will hope that many of the drowning caught the music and recalled the widely familiar hymn and were carried on the wings of its faith and its cry for mercy, into the presence of Him who deAres to be the Saviour of us all.

Board of Gukrdians' Sympathy.

At a meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians on Wednrsday, the newly-elected chairmsn (Mr E L Bent)ssid his first duty was to ask the Board to support him in expressing s3mpathy with those who had been deprived of breadwinners through the great sea tragedy which had rfceutly occurred. It ass a most serious thing fr this country and he could not help thinking about it morning snd night. A public body like that, which came into cruise, with certain classes of people, ought to show sympathetic hearts on such an occasion. The members of the Board then stood to signify concurrence with the expression of sympathy.

THE FANCY SIDE.

From the " Feathrred World."

As an exhibition fowl the Faverolles just now are very popular. There is a demand for them at very good prices, especially abroad. A personal friend of mine, and one who breeds Faverolles in a big way, tells me that he has no difficulty whatever in obtain- ing plenty of customers for as much stock as he can produce. Besides the salmon, we have the buff,ltlack, white and Cuckoo Faverolles. But these latter colcurs are more or less in an embryo stage. They are by no means perfect yet. The Whites are coming on rapidly though, and I have beard some people say that when better known they will press the White Orpington very bard for popularity. However, we must wait. Just let me say in passing, the Faverolles fowl is one that the amatuer might very well take up as an exhibition variety. And if one of the newer cclours are chosen you would stand a very good chance of coming to the front, because, seeing that they are not yet to perfection everyone, more or less, stands on the same level, or as the athlete would say, " We all start off scratch."

nousixa. is As in the affairs of human lir?, so in the poultry world, the question of housing is an impottant one. It is of no use purchasing valuable stock if they are badly housed, for good results will never come. Far better for fowls to rcost in trees than in broken-down and draughty roosting plains. And again, however substantially built the house may be—if erected in a damp place—this also is detrimental to successful poultry farming. The all-essential points are as follows : Well-built, plenty of light, perfect ventila- tion and kept dr. These are not many points to remember, but so very important are they that you should omit none. Whether it is more economical to purchase a fowl-house or to con- struct one must be decided by certain circumstances If you reside in a neighbourhood where timber is cheap, and easy of access, then it would be cheap-r to construct one yourself. Of course, lam presuming that you are handy with tools. It is somewhat strange, but nearly all lovers of poultry are hardy in this direction. Still farther, if yi u intend poultry-farming on a rather extensive scale, and all things being convenient, a great saving would be effected by pePsonally erecting the fowl-houses. If you adopt this course, don't be tempted to make the operations too cheap by using inferior and thin timber. Boards used for poultly houses should not be less than ;jla. thickness, the stouter the better; and when complete give them a good coating of tar. These are important i.ems, and well worth your closest observation.

===== 0002601_19120427_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
POULTRY HOUSE MAXERS.

There is a wonderful variety of poultry houses now on the market, and generally speaking I have nothing but praise to offer to many manufacturers now catering for the poultry pubic. If any complaint lies in the output of " shoddy " houses it is often the fault of the purchaser. Again I say, avoid cheapness. A poultry house may be scientifically built ; but if con- structed of cheap timbers it is a usele,s appliance,' and helps to spell failure in the poultry yat d. You must excuse me dwelling somewhat on this point, but it is necessary to do so. I have seen many a good-looking poultry house weather-beaten and twisted all shapes in a few weeks tme ; especially in bad weather. It is no use having a fancy wood-work iidging with a draught-hole in the side. In all poultry appliance catalogues you have the matter of choice between cheapness and quality. Choose the latter, it will pay you in the end.

===== 0002601_19120427_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
ILVICIUNG HINTS.

if you are purchasing eggs from a distance do not set them straight away ; they .shou'd be allowed to settle for a day bef rre placing under the hen. It is a good plan when possible to set two hens at the same time, so that if you only get moderate hatches, all could be given over to the care cf one hen ; thus saving extra trouble and attention. The period of incubation is twenty-one days. Do not be surprised if some of the chicks appear a day er t‘ro earlier or even a day or two later than this. Much depends upon the freshness of the eggs when set. When the eggs begin to chip do not tamper with them. Let nature have her course If a chicken is not strong enough to break through, the chances are that it would always be weak even if assisted by break- ing the shell. Chickens require no food for the first twenty-four hours of their existence, therefore I w( uld ask you to discard all "old woman methods" of giving them a drink ; it is cruel to d so. During the later stage of incubation they absorb the contents of the yolk-bag and this is sufficient to nourish them for the time stated. If the hen is of an uneasy disposition it might be a wise plan to remove each chick t save being trampled on. If so place them in a flannel- lined basket, keep them warm but not t ro hot. Having made sure that all have hatched out, clear the nest of broken shells and gently return the chicks to the hen. Then leave her to quietly settle down. In the mean- time you will have prepared a coop to receive the new family and when you are sure that the hen has settled, transfer them to the coop. They will have more freedom and less tisk of being trampled on. POULTRY FOR EXHIBITION

(Continued) SPECIALIST cLuns. When you have decided on the breed you intend to take up and when you have really made a start, join one of the Specialist Clubs Thesa societies are formed to look after and specially cater for the wants of the various exhibition breeds. The first advantage you gain is: You are at once given first band advice as to what points to breed for. Nearly all these clubs issue an illustrated standard of the breed catered for. There you can at otce consult the standard and keep yourself on the right track. It is a good plan to have the illustration hung up whore you can see it every time you sit down to have a meal or when you it to have a quiet smoke. By this method you practically graft into your mind, exactly what to breed for. Other advantages of membership are: You participate in the special prizes given by the club; and again, these clubs or nearly all, specially niter for the novice. They provide classes at different shows for novices only. These are classes provided for those who have never Nyon a prize at the big shows. Thus you see, that while you need have no fear of being beaten by the bigger men, you do have a chance of gradually 'getting on' amongst those who like your- self - are still in the novitiate. Membership fees are not heavy; usually varying from 2s. Gd. to 10s. Gd. per

CHILD'S REMARKABLE DEATH WHILE PLAYING.

The strange death ZFA-11;(11:t Edward Wolfries, aged four years, the s ,I 1 of William Wolfries, a miner, was investigated on Tuesday night at Crarulington, Northum- berland. 'the father said be found the boy suspended from the top of a military fence at the beat of a wash- house in the garden. The fence was Mt high and divided the garden from an adjoining field. The child was hanging by his left arm, which ass attached to the top of the fence by the wristband of the jersey and shirt. The feet were 12in. from the ground, and the jersey wal pressing round the neck. He bad been working in the garden while the was playing. The jury found that the chill was accidentally trangulated.

TURKISH DEFENCES ON THE DARDANELLES.

Last week the Italian Fleet, consisting of twody-iseven warships, appeared off the Dardanelles and opened fire on the forts. The Turkish forts at Kum Kaleh and Sedil Dahr replied, and the Fleet then withdrew. From an Italian source it is reported that

the Fleet's mission has terminated, its in- struction being neither to make a landing nor attempt the forcing of the Dardanelles. Our photo shows one of the big guns in the Kum Kaleh Fort et the mouth of the Dar- danelles.

MAY DAY.

NEXT WEEK'S FESTIVAL AT KNUTSFORD.

The quaint, old-world town of Knutsford u ill 'be the Mecca uf thou-ands of pilgrims from all parts of Lancsshire and Cheshire on Wednesday next when the annual May Day festival will be held. The time- honoured festival has taken place annually since 1864. It was organised by the Misses Clowes and Mr. G. W. Clarke, afterwards receiving the hearty support of the late respected Vicar of the Parish (the Rev. H. Barnacle, MA.), and continued under his direction until 1878, when it was taken cver by a representative Committee of Management, under whose care rapid improvements and attractions have been introduced, thus making it a delightful spectacle, and one attract- ing-to it spectatot s from afar. In 1887, the late King Edward VII., and the Queen Mother Alexandra, then Prince and Princess of Wales, witnessed and were highly pleased with the celebrations, and accepted from the May Queen of that year a bouquet, subse- quently giving to the Committee the title to use the prefix " Royal." Many new attractions are announced for this year's festival which promises to equal its long long line of predecessors in picturesqueneas and brilliance. The procession, which will be formed at the Town Hall at 2.15,wi1l be at least three-quarters of a mile in length and will include : Nursery rhymes in seven tableaux. England's cricketers in Australia, or " The return of the Ashes" in tableaux.

Argyle dancers. Irish bws and girls. Scotch boys and giils.

Welsh gills.

The Royal May Queen (Miss Edith Shaw) attended by a full court, guarded by the Royal Footguards. Boy Scouts will also be introduced on this occasion. Thanks to the generosity of the Canadian Pacific Railway Company the Dominion of Canada is to be adequately represented at the Festival. Agriculture being the basic industry of the dominion "Miss Canada " irr the hi y day of youth, is represented crowned with golden wheat ears and maple leaves and clad in a semi-classical robe of blue and green, emblematic of the radiance of the Canadian skies and the verdure of the vest expanse of arable land within her borders. A branch of maple leaves is in her right hand and she bolds a g Men cornucopia over-flowing with ripe fruits. She is seated on a Massey Harris Reaper, decorated with the Canadian fig, flowers and wheat sheaves. At her feet are a pile of different ores suggesting the mineral wealth of the Dominion. The primary industries of Canada are represented by five boys :—The farmer in his work-a-day garb, with hay rake over his shoulder ; the lumberman • stoutly clad with his axe ; the miner with pick-pack and pan ; the fisherman carrying his catch; The trapper with snow shoes and rifle. Before the lorry on which Canada is represented, rides a trooper of the North West Mounted Police typifying the reign .of law and order which this splendid body of men ensure for the settlers in the most remote districts of His Majesty's Premier Dominion. The May Queen will be crowned on the Heath at 3.30 and a most interesting programme will be gone through, including plaiting of the may pole, horn pipe, garland, clog and mr_rris dances "as danccd in ye olden time,' sword dance by Scottish Highlanders, hoop drill, &c.

THE GREAT INSURANCE

ACT.

MEDICAL BENEFIT.

BY DR. C. ADDISON, M.P.

Medical benefit means that insured persons during sickness will receive medical treatment and attendance, including the provision of necessary medicines and appliances, subject to the regulations which may be made by the Insurance Commissioners. The exact extent of the treatment, and the conditions under which it will be given, involve many con- siderations which are at present undeter- mined, and a consideration of which would not be proper in a general article. There are, however, three or four fundamental con- siderations which it may be an advantage to set out at this stage. MEDICAL BENEFIT & THE SICK FUND. In the first place, no scheme of national insurance against sickneal can be per- manently successful unless linked up with it there is an adequate and efficient medical service. It is obviously to the advantage of the societies that sick-pay shall be required by their members as little as possible ; that is to say, that the duration of illness shall be cut

as short as it can be by early recognition of the existence of the disease and by its prompt and efficient treatment. In this way only can the funds of the societies be guarded and in- creased, and the benefita which the members will be enabled to receive correspondingly improved. In other words, the ultimate suc- cess of the scheme depends upon its being worked in harmony with a medical service whose aim shall be not only the treatment but the prevention of disease. To obtain this very necessary and desirable result the hearty co-operation of the most capable men in the medical profession is plainly necessary, and every fair and reasonable effort should be made to secure it. The dosage of sick persons by bottles of medicine when they have found themselves ill is not the be-all and end-all of scientific medical service. It must seek to promote those habits of life which will enable people to resist the inroads of disease, to devise methods for its recognition at the earliest passible moment, and to prevent its spread. The more we can do this the more will be the additional benefits which societies will be able to give.

MEDICAL BENEFIT AND POVERTY. The more closely we investigate the origin at poverty the more we come to realise what an enormous proportion of it arises from the worker's loss of employment or earning power luring and after sickness. The lose of income which people suffer under these .circumstances :hives them into meaner and meaner lodgings. Their families have less fresh air, less gcod rood, less facilities for cleanliness and for the cooking of their food, so that the effects of the sickness spread far beyond the one who as afflicted, and often enough determine that his wife and children will themselves become subject to conditions of living which will make them less able-bodied, less able to maintain the struggle for existence and to re- ist inroads of disease. And so you find that the children from one-room tenements at, say, the average age of eleven, will be four or five inches shorter in height, and ten or eleven pounds lighter in weight, than chil- dren of the same average age living in four- room tenements. That is to say, the sickness of the father and resulting impoverishment often means that his children will be lets able to take advantage of 'education, and more likely themselves in the future to become a burden on the Poor Law. .

A few weeks ago I had the opportunity of investigating the conditions of the families of the names of those on the list of the relieving officer for a certain district in London during one week in February. There were fifty-three names on the list, twenty-three of them i being there for medical relief. On investigating these cases, after making full allowance for the effects of drink, of improvident and thriftless habits, it is quite certain that nearly :Ili of them would have kept them- selves clear of the Pooi. LaNy had it not been for the occurrence of sickness. CLUB PRACTICE.

The medical benefit under the Insurance Act is to be administered by the Insurance Committees for the • counties and county boroughs, and it will not be carried on on the lines of the club practice as we have hitherto known it. A very little reflection will con- vince us that this change in administration is absolutely necessary if we are to obtain a medical service on proper national lines.

In an average street in a working-class dis- trict there will probably be one or more in- sured persons in every house. The man from No. 1 may be an Oddfcllow, from No. 2 a Forester, from No. 3 a member of the Hearts of Oak, from No. 4 a member of a dividing society, from No. 5 a member of a trade union, and so on—that is to say, the insured persons in this street. will belong to a large number of different societies with different offices and with no communication between one another. It may so happen that several persons In this street might be ill at the same time from the same or from different causes. Unless it was notifiable as an in- fectious disease, all the records would go to the different societies' offices, which knew nothing of orie another, and nobody would be the wiser. On the other hand, it might easily happen that workers from a large number of different streets, or even different districts, employed at the same factory or shop, were ill at the same time, or showed an excessive amount of illness. Some improper conditions of sanitation, of ventilation, or otherwise, might be responsible for this prevalence of sickness in this street or factory. What- ever may be the cause, it is manifest that if we are to obtain proper machinery for the prevention of the spread of disease all these records of its distribution and its occurrence, and of the abode and employ- ment of those affected, must be made avail- able at one centre, and that to allow them to be scattered „in fifty different offices will be, to set up at the very beginning a system which by its very nature is hopelessly in- efficient. The society and the sick member have one interest in the matter of disease, and that is that they shall get the best at- tendance they can at the earliest possible moment, and with as much freedom for themselves as possible: At the present time, in the ordinary sick club, a member must either have the doctor appointed by the club or pay someonejelse on his own .account. Under the Insueknee Act it is arranged that, as far as is reasonably possible, there shall be freedom of choice between the doctor and the patients.

The regulations under which arrangements will be made by medical practitioners to attend insured persons have not yet been drawn up, and there is no advantage in this article in discussing the details. A great deal,. of very unfortunate misunderstanding- has arisen over this question, and the Commis- sioners have a difficult task in seeking to de- vise arrangements which shall be fair and satisfactory both to the medical profes-Aston, the insured persons, and the taxpayer. An adequate and hearty medical service, and one which will attract good men, is plainly neces- sary, and proper remuneration and condi- tions of service must therefore he provided; btit neither this nor any other Government could possibly dnd in the House of Com- mons or before the public any demands for additional remuneration which were not sup- ported by sufficient evidence to show that they were necessary.

JUMPED FROM RAILWAY WINDOW.

IsZeir Cooksbridge, on the L.B, and S.C. Railway on Thursday, a young man travelling in a carriage with some ladies on the 1.25 train to Eastbourne, suddenly threw himself out of the window. He was dead when found. A stick in his possession was engraved, "P. C. Henderson, Lovetts, MossoHong." At Loughborough on Thursday, children playing on a rubbish beep unearthed the dead bodies of two babies

There were two events JUSTICE FOR of great national WALES. importance on Tues- day. One was the hearty and unanimous adoption of the Home Rule Bill by the Irish National Convention, 'and the other was the intioduction of the Welsh Disestablishment Bill by the Home Secretary, Mr. MCKENNA. These two measures, coming thus into conjunction, as it were, like two great planets in the political sky, carry the mind back to the time when Mr. JOSEPH CHAMBERLAIN separated himself from the Liberal" Party on the ground of his disagreement with Mr. GLADSTONE'S first Home Rule Bill. In the first fervid apostleship of his dissentient Unionism, the member for West Birmingham made a strong appeal to Welsh feeling and sentiment. He pointed out that the Home Rule Bill must distract attention from other measures of great importance, and that in particular, it must put off indefinitely the realisation of the desire of the Welsh I people for the Disestablishment of the Church in Wales. What was predicted has no doubt occurred. What again becomes a burning question to-day is som,2thing that might have been disposed of many years since if there had been no question of Irish Home Rule before the country. It is because of that close and intimate knowledge of the subject which comes of being a Welsh member rather than because he is HOME SECRETARY, that Mr. MCKENNA has been placed in charge of the Welsh Dis- establishment Bill. As he lost no time in proving, he is thoroughly equipped with the fullest possible knowledge of his subject,alike in regard to principles andsletails. He follows Mr. ASQUITH, who has twice introduced a Welsh Disestablishment Bill to the House of Commons,with the result of a second reading in the first instance. [Mr. MCKENNA'S measure'does not greatly differ from its pre- decessors, but it is supported by new facts. Since the present Government came into power a Royal Commission has been inquir- ing into the subject, and Mr. MCKENNA was able to deal with the statements made in its report. One of them is that the Welsh branch of the Church Establishment is responsible for about one-fourth of the religious activity of the Principality. This, as is obvious, makes it exceedingly difficult to maintain that the Welsh Establishment is in any true sense a National Church. Various tests were applied by the Royal Commission, and, as Mr. MCKENNA con- tended, they all led up to the same con- clusion. So far as numerical strength goes it was established beyond dispute that

the Church of England includes only a rela - tively small minority of the actively religious people of Wales. There was a time when the Church of England in Wales embraced within its fold the whole of the Welsh people. All that has been changed. Apply- ing the tests of vitality and energy, said Mr* MCKENNA, the Welsh section of the Estab- lished Church—there is no independent Establishment in Wales, it should be under- stood—on the most liberal computation does not include one-third of the religious life of the country.We need not go into the particu- lars as to the accommodation provided in the churches of the Principality by the Establishment and the other religious bodies. They all bear out the conten- tion of the HOME SECRETARY. The con- clusion was that the Establishment has provided only one-sixth of fhe accommoda- tion that has been required. The Church of England in Wales, the Home Secretary maintained, has no right whatever to be regarded as a National Church. It is repudi- ated by the bulk of the Welsh people. In two recent General Elections thirty-one members out of thirty-four have been returned to support Disestablishment and Disendowment. Wales has, in fact, never been so much of one mind on any subject as for a long time past it has been on this. There has been no such strong case, said Mr. MCKENNA, except that which resulted in the Disestablishment of the Church in Ireland.

There has been much exaggeration, as will be seen, as to the extent to which Disendow - ment is to be carried. Instead of a merely fractional part of the endowments remaining in the hands of the Welsh Church, under the Government Bill, precisely one-third will be returned. Even as to this, of course, we may expect a very stiff fight. The Church always fights well and with great desperation. There will, too, we believe, be more sincerity and conviction behind the opposition to Welsh Disestablishment than there is behind the opposition to Home Rule. There is no Ulster in Wales, as Mr. MCKENNA said, but the proposals now made will meet with the most determined resistance, not only from Welsh, but from English Churchmen. The battle that is now to be joined will, in fact, be one of those political events which make history in the absolute sense.

The Tory party have TOWN HALL WARD received a set-back in ELECTION. Town Hall Ward which

they fully deserved. In a sense the election proved more of a defeat for Toryism than a triumph for Liberalism, as Liberal politics were not at stake. Coun- cillor Lindley stood as a Liberal nominee, but he did not concern himself with party political questions at all, and bluntly stated that "he absolutely declined to recognise imperial politics in municipal affairs." Nevertheless the Tory party secured a candidate to their liking, and proceeded to rim the election on the lines of a Parlia- mentary contest. Among other foolish deeds—which, no doubt, left their impression on the minds of the soberer section of the electorate—they issued a handbill inviting the electors to "ask Mr. Lindley whether he is going to suppoit the Church or the Radical Party on the Disestablishment Bill." Apparently the electors had too much sense to concern themselves with any such ques- tion in selecting a man to take his part (and their's) in the good government of the town Mr. Lindley fought and won as a citizen, while the Tories fought and lost as a political party seeking entirely political and selfish ends. So may it always be

When first the sugges- NATIONAL tion was made that one HEALTII WEEK. week in the year should be devoted to the con- sideration of public health there were a few adverse criticisms. Some people said that a week was not long enough to be of any use and that fifty-two weeks per annum were scarcely long enough. But after all, experience shows that the public mind can- not be working all the time at one problem. One cannot have an eternal Sabbath in this work-a-day world—some don't want one. But that is no reason why one should be irreligious 6 days out of 7. And so it is with health. The originators of the idea, the Agenda Club, have laid it down that the whole subject should be treated in a non- controversial manner. And surely it is a topic upon which men and women of all sects and parties may discover common ground and be all the better for the discovery. It has sometimes been urged that in all progressive movements for the betterment of the people it is well-nigh impossible to reach the most ignorant. But even if the filtration of ideas is slow it is none the less certain. Fashions of all kinds begin with the best, but finally reach the worst. The harem skirt will eventually be 'found upon 'Arriet at Appy 'Ampstead, when she will cease to use the skipping rope on Bank Holidays. The excessive drinking of Georgian times that was popular among the upper classes lingers among the workingclass, but it is rapidly dying out. And health ideas will also penetrate in the same way. The cold bath each year reaches larger masses of the people. The whole attitude of the public towards tuberculosis is changing with startling

rapidity. Ten years ago people conderal to certain death the children of consumPt parents. To-day the idea of heredity passing, while that of infectivity is tak its place. Formerly a consumptive pati lay down to die, as hopelessly as a wouill Kaffir. Nowadays the patient sets him cheerfully to lead the simple life and I:11 tuberculosis in the category of conquer' evils.

There is every reason why the Pul should take an active part in the battlo health. Public health is the child of refc and. revolution. Dr. Leslie Mackenzie poi

out how the popular interest in public hea dates from 1832, the year of the great Ref° Bill. And it is not a little singular that I leaders of the movement, men like Chadwi Bain, Grotes and the two Mills were 3 medical men but laymen. It would be ful to lininimise the enormous services of technically trained in the! health fig Without their guidance conquest would impossible. But it is well to remember ti now, as ever, it is the business of the peel

to declare war on dirt and squalor and dis• and to sustain the fight by their enthusi. and their material aid. The enthusiasm 1 stays its hand before it reaches the pock not of much avail.

MOTOR CYCLES.—Agent for " Douglas "S; "Premier.' New Models now in stock. Trials and all information free.-J. S. GARLAND, 51-55, Bridge at., Warrington.-Advt Death of Mr. James Cassidy. Another familiar figure has been removed by the death, which occurred on Sunday, at 13, Vinare- str eet, Rowley, of Mr. James Cassidy, who was in his 92nd year. Mr. Cassidy belonged to an old Warrington family, which is remarkable for its longevity. His father lived to the age of 93 years. and a brother, Mr. James Cassidy, battle manufacturer, recently celebrated his SOth birthday. The late Mr. James Cassidy had been ill for abrut three months, but before Christmas he was in full pos.ession of Iris faculties, and was surprisingly vigorous and active. Two or three years ago he fell while descending the stairs, and it is possible that the mishap may have hastened his end. Mr. Cassidy served his apprentice- ship with the firm of Messrs. Peter Stubs, Ltd., which he left when a young man to commence in business on his own account, as a file cutter. He retired a few years ago. The Territorials. - - - • • - • •

The Battalion South Lancashire Regiment began the season's training rn Saturday afternoon with a route march. A strong muster of officers and men fell in at tlss Drill Hall under the command of Lieutenant Cclonel B. Fairclough Other officers on parade included Maj r F. M. Appleton, Captain and Adjutant Elgood, CantAn G. R. Cr, sfie!cl, Cap'ain Leyton and Captain R. Smith. Sixtcen officers and 340 other ranks were present. Shortly after 330 the order to march was given and the men, headed by the new drum and fife band and the Battalion band, proceeded to Penketh. After a brief halt, a return to the town was made, and the battalion was dismissed in front of the Town Hall. Excellent progre-s is being made with recruiting, and only fifty-three men are now required to make up the full establishment strength. - - - -

Cheshire Lines Platelayers' and Workmen's Prorident Society.

The thirty-seventh annual meeting of this society, which consists of the workmen employed in the Engineer's Department of the Cheshire Lines Railway, was held at the works, 13attersby-lane, Warrington. The chair was taken by Mr. Harry Vanden, M. Inst., C.E., the Chief Engineer of the Company, and the meeting was largely attended by officials and delegates from Lancashire and Cheshire. The Secretary submitted the annual report, which stated that the number of members was 1,085. A large sum had been expended in aid of 274 sick members, who had received assistance from the funds of the society, and 20 death claims had also been paid. The report and statement of accounts baying been passed, Mr. Thomas Pratt was re-elected secretary for the ensuing year and Messrs. G. Mather and R. Jones were elected, auditors.

The Pleturedrome and Health Week.

The programme for next week at the Picturedrome is particularly attractive and at the request of the local cororni•tee of the National Health Week some special subjects will be shown at eaeo performance dealing with health.On Monday, Tuesday an:: Wednes- day "The awakening of John Bond," a picture specially produced by the Edison film company with the co-operation of the American National Association for the Prevention of Tuberculosis, will be the star film and will be found not only of high dramatic value but of great educational interest. The chief picture for the week end will be "The traitress of Barton's Court" depicting Low Sally, a coster girl, living in Barton's Court, sees a robbery with violence com- mitted and gives information agoinst the culprit. By so doing she incurs the enmity of the neighbours who endeavour to murder her in the room. She fires the roof to give the alarm and is eventually rescued by her fireman lover.

The Late Mr. George Ratcliffe. The funeral of the late Mr. George Ratcliffe, brush maker, of Golborne-street, took place at the Cemetery on Sunday afternoon. The cortege left Chester-road and proceeded to St. Paul's Church where the first part of the burial service was held, the Revs. W. liracecamp, 11. V. Pigot and E. E. Peters taking part. In addition to the church choir there were present the members of the Warrington Male Voice Choral Union and representatives of the Late Spring Rose Lodge, I with the district officers of the Loyal Order of Ancient I Shepherds, Ashton Unity. Mr. Wilkes was at the organ and played "Blest are the departed" and "Be thou faithful unto death " before the service began and the He'd March in " Saul "at the close. The members of St. Paul's choir, the Male Voice Choral Union aed Order of Shepherds preceded the cortege to the Cemetery. At the grave side the Male Voice Choir sang, "The long day closes" (Sullivan). Numerous floral tributes were received from members of tlo choir and friends. Mr and Mrs J F Awbery, until recently roaster and matron of the Warring,on Workhouse, left Central Station on Monday by the 12.55 train, en route . to Lucerne, where they are to reside. They were accom- panied by Dr Taylor, late resident medical officer of the Workhouse. As rim train steamed out of the station a cheer was raised.

Colborne-street Baptist Church,

Preaching on Sunday evening at the Golborne-street Baptist Church the Rev H Ogden took for his subject "The Titanic and its lessons." He said that bad the liner gone 100 miles south or had she crept into the danger zone instead of racing and had she been content to lose a day, she would probably be sailing the ocean yet. One curious and interesting conjecture was raised in the mind. Was it the ordered intent of God that all the lives which were lost on that liner should have been called to their doom just at that hour? Round that question there was an eternal Mystery. The lesson to be learned was that in the midst of life they were in death. An incident revealed a company with a hand- ful of cards and the Angel of Death looking over their shculders. We learned indeed in most distressing form that there was a Sovereignty over and above man and that over-confidence in dealing with nature might be man's ruin. The boat was rogarded as unsinkable ; the luxury of the aristocrat and the respectability of the toiler were combined in the ship. How many years of careful nursing and how much of the national treasury had been expended upon each unit, and all that had been lost to pay the price of suffering ? For whom had the racing on the Atlantic been arranged ? Commercial rivalry was largely to blame but the racing on the ocean was not for the steerage passengers ; they would have to wait until the millennium ere commercial enterprise would make racing for their benefit. The list of sur- vivors showed that 63 per cent of the first-class passengers were saved and 73 per cent of the steerage passengers were lost. All those catastrophes revealed to them with painful monotony th .t it wasthe unknown toiler who ultimately paid the highest price. The blue rihand of the Atlantic, said the preacher in conclusion, onset give way to the safety of man.

The Examiner, Saturday. April, 27 1912. SHAKESPEAREAN COMMEMORATION.

PERFORMANCES AT ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

RICHARD HI. AND " JULIUS CESAR."

The great and widespread interest shewn in the preparations, wlock for a considerable time have been proceeding. for the production of these two great tragedies of Shakespeare's in commemoration of his birth, indicAtes the steady growth of an improved taste in matters dramatic in this, our ancient town of Warrington. It cannot have been otherwise than beneficial to the large number of people who have been engaged for weeks in the process of assimilating the text and imbibing the spirit of t: ese w rks of genius, and whatever the effect on the auditors at the public performances slay be, all those who have participated in th- reheivsals have imindised themselves in these great plays, and, no doubt, have received infinite stimulations from them. Under this aspect, alone, the indebtedness to Mr. Charles D. Law, the promoter and organizer of this movement, cannot be too highly estimated. But there is a further good %ilia may, I think, he looked for se a result.of the increased interest in the plays of Shakespeare, which this movement his evoked. It should cause the educated public to extend to the theatre a more, consistent patronage, to induce a greater care for the sumiesses of the stag., and en honest inquiry into its failures ; to seziure adequate support for really elevating performances, and to make managers and ctors fed that they are not dependent for appreciation of their efforts on the verdict of frivolous patrons sho prefe-r brainless productions in endless succession. The better the stage, the better the town, and it should be the endeavour of all good, earnest townsmen who desire the intellectual improvement of their fellows to use their influence in encouraging the taste for a better class of entertainment at the theatre and to give their whole-hearted support to the management when it is provided.

RICHARD 111.

To writing this play, Shekespear was largely / influenced by Hi • grandi( se style of M rrlowe with whom he had been r ssoeiated in the writing of other historical plays. This is es id, rweil by its almost total absence of pathos end the exclusion of prose in its compo-ition. But Shakespeare's Is ger compass is clearly shown. His liichord as humour and irony. He becomes a villain from choice, as a compensatioo for his want of the personal ivivantages enjoye I b, others, and he carries out his assumed !Ole with the greatest gusto, never faltering in dealing. the c'ea thy stroke, jesting gaily with his victitus hf reliant], and expressing obviously unfeigned a imiratioi, of them afterwards. All p evi,m4 illaiiis cf the stage had talked in tremendous high sounding terms— out-Heroding Herod. Shakespe ‘re wakes his Richard easy and collcquial, enaging, displaying when he chooses qualities which make his absolute call usness the more terrific. It has been customary to refer to " Hamlet" as the play of all others which would be meaningless were the part of Hamlet himself left out. Bat" Richard III." is almost ! more truly a one man play than Hamlet. The gigantic I figure of Richard himself dominates everywhere —the rest, even Richmond, to whose forces he succumbs, are pigmies in comparison and the whole dram t till near the close is an all but unbroken monotony of easy triumphs over victims who w old be helpless if they were not blind. Even Buckingham, Richard's comrade and ally, is lost from the moment he hesitates. There is but one exception to the universal het lessness and that is the hapless woman whose position, as a banished enemy, the defenceless survivor of a ruined cause, might seem to make helplessness complete. But Margaret of Anjou, the still vital soul of the ruined House of Lancaster, is still terrible t Richard at the height of his power —he quails visibly before her—for in this forlorn shadow speaks the voice of the Nemesis which is striking down the enemies of her house by Richard's hand and which will last of all strike down Richard himself.

THE UTILE PRINCES, MISSES DOROTHY AND MIDDY TRUSLOVE.

The presentation of this play on Thursday evening with each elaboratioa of detail as to make it worthy to be •Olassed with any of the great revivals by tbe greatest professional actors and managers which have occurred during the lifetime of the writer—at least in respect of scenery, dresses and appointments together with the general arrangement of the business of the play, and in acting, suffering nothing by com- parison with many professional, or with the best of anything previously seen in smateur performances, reflects the very highest honour and credit upon the gentleman to whom we owe the formation of the Shakespearean Dramatic Society and whose ind efatig- able zeal and patient an I painstaking exerti one in drilling and coaching had resulted in such a magni- ficently successful pro Motion. Mr. Charlss D. Law and his work cannot be overpraised Indeed, I am conscious of a difil.tulty in ende ,vouring to do justice to its true worth.

The play had been juliciously cat for acting by Mr. Law but all the principal teenes were given. At the opening of the play we have a very fine street scene representing old London and the assembling and passing of male and female citizens

play, and quite I.fted it above its surroundings, Of the mate parts, it is ni t easy to speak without appearing to make invidious diatinctions. Mr.a.lohn Lowe as the Duke of Clarence, spoke and acted with discretion and gave a most feelirg interpretation of the well-known description of Clarence's dream in the Tower. The Duke of Buckingham was plated by Mr. Harvey 0. Adams who give a most careful and con- sis•ent rendering of this difficult and somewhat thank- less part. Mr. W. H. Brown as Lord Hastings was alert enough in his manner of acting but was, we thought, a ,mewhat stolid, if not altogether stilted in his manner of speaking. Mr. H. M. Ashton as King Edward, I think deserves great praise for his careful readering. It showed a most tender appreciation of the emoticns of the King, with a strict regard for the poetry of the part. Mr. Leslie L Langford made a fine commanding figure as Richmond, and played his part well. His speech was manly, and his words enunciated clearly and with expression. We welcome this gentleman as a strong addition to the company. Many other parts are deserving of mention, but space prohibits other than a general tribute of praise to all the ladles 11nd gentlemen who have so cheerfully and enthusiastically helped to make the production E 33 complete a success. For Mr. Charles Law himself, as we have said, it is impossible to speak too highly. He is the mainspring of the action and without him we feel that the play wculd be a mere pageant. His conception of the part of Richard is a most inte.esting ore and he enacts it most con- sistently throughout. Mr. Law's Richard is a masterful firiure—more so, I think, in many respects, than Shakespeare made him, but the play as presented is the most masterly example of stage lepreeentation that has been seen in Warrington. We have wished that the Warrington public hid proved themselves more appreciative by crowding every part of the theatre to see it.

TOWN HALL WARD ELECTION. A LIBERAL VICTORY.

Air. Firth Lindley (L.) Mr. Joseph Banks (C.)

Majority 26 The result of the election in Town Hall Wald on Thursday to fill the vacancy created by the death of Councillor William Rogers, was a victory for Mr. Firth Lindley, the Liberal nominee, by 26 votes. Never haa a keener municipal contest taken place—in fact the excitement thr ughout the day was retnini- scant of a general election. Each side bad a strong candidate with business interests in the ward, Mr. Firth Lindley, who made such a splendid fight against the late Councillor Rogers in 1910, having again been prevailed upon to stand in the Liberal interest, while Mr. Joseph Banks came foi ward as the Conservative candidate. In view of the fact that the work of the Town Council is confined to local administration, Mr. Lindley did not raise any political issues, but the Conservatives determined that, as far as they were concerned, the election should be fought on strong party lines. They issued a handbill inviting the electors t " ask Mr. Lindley ,vhether he is going to support the Church i or the Radical party on the Disestatlishaent Bill. He cannot have it both ways. Ycur duty is to vote for Banks." To , this Mr. Lindley replied with an address in which he stated : " I absolutely decline to recognize imperial politics in municipal affairs. If elected by you, I shall, to the utmost of my ability, serve the best interests of the ratepayers irrespective of party." The Conservative retort was that Mr. Lindley was standing as a Liberal candidate, selected by the Liberal executive, and the electors were asked to " show your resentment of these tactics by voting for Banks.' Another handbill compared the assessments on which the candidates pay rates, and concluded: " Vote for Banks, the largest ratepayer." The polling statirn was at the Kendrick-street school, and animated scenes were witnessed in the vicinity. Each side had a strong body of workers and a gaol supply of c ,nveyances. During the after- noon Sir Gilbert Greenall Bart„ and Lady Greenall visited the Conservative committee room. DECLARING THE RESLTL At the close of the poll the ballot boxes were cc nveyed to the Town Hall and the votes were counted in the large committee room, in the presonce of the returning officer (Alderman Smethurst), th e Mayor (Councillor Dr. Joseph), Al ierman Pierpoint d numerous supporters of both can lida'es Ii less thin half an hour the result was known, Alderman S.nethurst declar- ing the flgu-es as given above. Councillor LINDLEY said it tras his pleasure and privilege to prr,pme a hearty v. ti or thirds to the rt turning officer, the presiding officer an I poll clerks and all concerned in the electi: n. That was his scolid contest and ',either of them bad b?en very serious e ffiirs from the personal point (f vii w He thought his opponent would agree tz at they h td hid a very pleasent light. (Hear, hour). Mr BANKS, in s conding, add,,d h's tribute to the work which had been done by the wl ole of the Alderman SMKTIIURsT rammed I hanky. His dntiee, he said, had been very light. They had had no better or more comfortable fleht in the Town Hall Ward. 'l he returning r than proceeded to the Towni Hall steps and announced the result to a fairly large crowd, hearty cheers being raised for the victor.

AT THE LIBERAL CLUB. Accompanied hy an enthusiastic haled of supporters, Councilor Lindley then proceeded to the Liberal Cliab, where several congratulatt ry speeches were delivered Mr P PEACOCK (chairman of the Liberal Executive) said he vane proud to announce the first vc,ory obt lined since be took up the chairmanship of the Liberal Association. (Applause). If the workeis we uld only emulate their action that day at the general election, they would have no difficulty in re tuning ev Liberal for Wa-rington. (Applause). Despite all the it tlaence ot Mr Harold Smith having been in Warrington this week, Sir Gilbert and Lady Greenall and the children I raving visited the ear d that day, and the two brevaeries havit g sent conveyances, the Liberals bad beaten them with clean hands. PALACE AND -HIPPODROME.

Another of those excellent programmes which War- rington audiences eagerly anticipate has been sub- mitted at the Hippodrome during the week, and the audiences have been exceptionally large at each performance. The Jackson Family have bad a most enthusiastic reception, which is fully deserved, for their turn is undoubtedly one of the best and most entertaining musical performances we have been privileged to witness for some time. They have a perfect control over the numerous instruments they play, and the applause which they earned was most deafening. An encore on Monday was loudly demanded, and the . four artistes responded in their usual brilliant manner. Another musical treat is provided by Edna Lyall, who was warmly received, and highly pleased the audience with her magnificent voice and cultivated vocalisation. Ara, Zebra, and Vora are highly successful in their novelty turn," Indians on the Prairie," and some splenc'id acrobatic feats are accomplished. Harry Fields gives us some very realistic impersonations of popular music-hall stars in a new and pleasing way, his best e erects being those of the mimicry of ilkie Bard, Charlie Whittle, and Alec Miley. George Payne's Wanderers score in their pretty roadside scene in which whistling, singing and dancing are intro- duced with advantage. The tramp causes much laughter with his absurdities and the whole turn is thoroughly.worthy of the large amount of apprecia- tion which the audience unstintingly give. Ada Gatley ia a clever wire artiste and expert Juggler and Katie Marsh is a sparkling comedienne, with a bright and cheery style which Warrington people delight to see. Mario gives a really clever Juggling entertainment in which he introduces some new tricks, and a certain amount of sensationalism, with marked succe s. " JULIUS CESAR."

Thoughtful readers of Dickens will call to mind the innocent enjoyment David Copperfield derived from his first visit to the theatre and will recall tlv3 enthusiastic expression of his feelings to his friend Steerforth cn rerewing the acquaintance of their boyhood at the Olden Cross Hotel David had ben t ) Covent Garden Theatre and bad there seen" Julius Ctosar." _

" To have, ' he says," all those noble Romans alive before me and walking in and out for my entertain- ment, instead of being the stern task-masters they had been at school, was a m, st novel and delightful effect. But the mingled reality and mystery of the whole chow, the influence upon me of the poetry, the lights, the music, the c mpany ; the smooth, stupendous changes of glittering and brilliant scenery were so dazzling, and opened up such illimitable regions of delight that when I came out into the rainy street I felt as if I bad come from the clouds, where I had been leading a romantic life for ages, t a bawl- ing, splashing, umbrella struggling, coach jostling, muddy, miserable world." And the laughing reply of the superior Steerforth, who had already tappe I most of the gay pleasures of city life,and had no enthu-iasno for the romantic and pletic, came as a cold douche upon the youthful ecstacies of David. My dear young Davy—you are a very daisy. The daisy of the fiel I at sunrise, is cot fi ostler than you are. I have been at Covent Garden, too, and there never was a more miserable business."

The attitude of mind—the spirit in which we go ti the theatre is here shown to be the pivot on which our enjoyment depends—whether we shall be edified and uplitted or bored and depressed. In my own mind I am convinced that we can, if we will, always, to our great advantage end delight, keep up the enthusiasm of David Copperfield, and from boyhood to old age retain the theatre icrour associations as a palace of fancy. Ttie play of " Julius Coe sac, as almost every school boy has been taught, is founded on one of the great 'events in ths world's history, the fall of the Roman Republic and the rise of the Roman Empire, that Empire so long the dominant power of the ancient world and whose influence is so deeply felt even in our modern life. The lesson of "Julius Cresar " is that vengeance, death shall follow rebellion for insufficient cause, for misjudging the political stato of one's country, and misjudging the means—taking unlawful ones—to attain our ends. Brutus is the hero of the play, yet Cre, ar's spirit rules, as Cassius and Brutus, 'before their deaths, acknowledge. His bodily ptesenee is weak and con- temptible but his spirit rises, arms his avengers, and his assassins proclaim its might. Brutus is one of Shakespeare's noblest men, but greatly wanting in practical knowledge and in his judgment of men. The remarks already made on Mr. Law's production of "Richard III." apply with increased force to the setting in which "Julius Crosar " is presented. The scenery throughout is appropriate in character and most effective, Ciosar's house being a particularly fine example of a Roman interior. The Senate and the Foium also are splendid

specimens of the scene painter's art, the latter scene being made most realistically impressive by Cwsar's funeral pyre which is set up before the pulpit from which Brutus and Antony harangue the populace. Unquestionably this scene is a triumph of stage realism, the body of citizens, swayed by the oratory of Brutus and afterwards by that of Antony (whose moving eloquence turns them from a belief in the justice of Cresar's "taking off," to rage and mutiny against his murderers) being the most genuinely earnest and deeply ii.teresting body of " supernumeraries " it has ever been my good fortune to see upon any stage—not excepting Henry Irving's wonderful "crowds." This is not due to the superiority of the " material " alone, but Cto the wonderful organ- izing ability of Mr. Law, who has instilled them with his own enthusiasm for the realization of the'.; desired effect.

It is quite impossible to do more than give a general meed of praisa to the ladies and gentlemen who have sustained the principal paits in this gieat play. It would be fitting to make, and deserves, individual mention of each one, but the hour at which it is necessary to go to press puts it quite out of my power to do so. Mr. Harold Smith, as Mark Antony, is made the popular figure in the performance and with .the audience. His acting and his fine robust elocution are good to see and listen to. But there are others. Mr. John Lowe, as the impulsive Cassius is very good. Mr. Harvey 0 Adams as .Cmaar gives a worthy rendering ci the part. Mr. Ashton also as Caeca is quite at home in his contemptuous descrip- tion of the refusal of the Crown by Ca-sar and the b-haviour of the citizens. Mr. W. H. Brown figures well in the part of Octavius Cmsar. Miss Molly Fairclough gives a very fine rendering of Portia, Brutus's wife, arid Miss Tunstall as Calpurnia is satisfactory. Mrs. Law plays the part of Lucius very well indeed. Mr. Law himself is the Brutus and gives a very worthy rendering of the pait. Should an opportunity occur next week I rimy revert to a con- sideration of the acting of the individual parts which now has to be dealt witlian cursorily. Success ! is to be inscribed on the commemoration performances this year.

WEDDING AT LATCHFORD. Fairclough— Briggs.

There was considerable local interest in the needing which to 1 place quietly at .1030 on Wulnesday morning in Ciirist Church, Latchford, of Mr Harry Fairclough, second son of Mr H T Fair- dough, Barryfield, Latchford, and Miss Elsie Marian Rh. odds Briggs, third daughter of the late Mr Thomas Briggs, of Stoneleigh, Salford. 13,th the I 1 bride and bridegroom are well-known and esteemed in Latchford, and many relatives and friends assembled to witness the marriage ceremony, which was con- ducted by the Rev 0 C Briggs, vicar of Chr'st Church and brother of the bride, and the Rev H J Firth, vic ir-designate of Hellifield, brother-in-law. The 113mn " Oh perfect love" was sung, and Ur T L Fry, the organist, played Mendelssohn's " Wedding March." The bride was given away by her brother, Mr Percy C Briggs, of Priory Bank, Sale, and cariied a beautiful bouquet cf white lilies and tulirs, the gift of the bridegroom. Her bridesmaid was her little niece, Miss Mary Briggs, daughter of the vicar. The best man was Mr. Eric Fairclough, brother of the bridegroom. A large number of choice presents was received, includ- ing a silver salver from the Master Builders of Warrington, and a Georgi3n teapot, cream jug, and soup ladle from the employees at the office and works of the bridegroom in Warrington.
