CYCLING NOTES.

THE WARRINGTON CLUB. This Club opened its active season on Saturday evening last with a hot pot and stoking conceit at headquarters, Patten Arms Hotel. The fixture was ' fairly well attended and proved in every way very enjoyable. After an excellent supper prepared by Mr. Burleigh, which received ample justice, a musical programme was gone through and was much appreciated. The opening item, a double duet "The morn hath raised her lamp above" rendered by Messrs. Draper, Hassall, Fieldhouse and Poole, earned for them well deserved applause. Mr P 0 Bailey in disposing of his songs "0 silver stream" and "Island of dreams" showed himself to be a bass of great promise. Mr J Draper, tenor, distinguished himself in his songs "The message" and "The stars of Normandy," the latter bringing for him an encore which he responded to with "My Queen." Mr ti H Smith gave "My Pipe," a musical monologue which was well received. A Somersetshire song, "On a January morning," rendered by Mr. Fred Fieldhouse, brought for him an encore, and his " Soldier's toast" in response, gave members present an opportunity of displaying their vocal abilities in a pleasing chorus. A humorous quartette, " Four jolly sailor men," by Messrs. Fieldhouse, Pole, Bailey and Smith," was rendered in true nautical fashion. Two songs by Mr. J. B. Poole, "Son of Mine" and "The rebel," showed him to have lost none of his reputation as an accpmplishei bass soloist. A second. double quartette, "Watchman, what of the night? " by Mess's. Draper, Hassall, Fieldhouse and Poole," was beautifully rendered. Mr. S. Hassall, with "Come into the garden, Maud," shewed himself to great advantage as a tenor with sweetness of tone. A quartette, " When evening's twilight gathers round," by Messrs. Draper, Hassall, Fieldhouse and Poole was a fitting termination to a very enjoyable programme. The very successful evening was brought to a close by a vote of thanks to the artistes, and the accom- panist (Mr. Percy Smith), ably moved by Mr. W. Litton, secondei by Mr. H. Hope and responded to by Mr. J. B. Poole.

. _ The opening run is to be held to-day to Hunt's Cross, where bowling will form part of the afternoon's arrangements. '1 he section is to leave headquarters at 230 p.m. Unfortunately owing to the curtailed train service, members will not be able to take advantage of a train usually run from War- rington at 2.21 p.m. The opening of the bowling season at Sankey Chapel House will take place on Wednesday next aud subject to a continuance of the present favourable weather, a large muster of members is expected.

DEATH OF MRS. LUKE ELLISON.

A well known and highly respected Warrington lady has passed away during the week in the person of Alia Ellison wife of the late Alderman Luke Ellison. The deceased, who was 75 years of age, had been in ill- health for so:ne time but had been able to go out until Wednesday last week, when she was confined to her bed with heart trouble, a complaint she has suffered from for some time. She was attended by Dr Robinson at the residence of her son-in-law, Laburnum Villas, Latchford, where her demise took phee on Tnesd,y morning. She was, up to recently, connected with the Wesleyan Church,Latchford and had been a hard worker for the Wesleyans of Latchford. Her husband was largely instrumental in getting that sacred edifice erected. She leaves two sons and two daughters. The funeral took place on Thursday at the Grapprit- ball Parish Church where a large number of sympa- thisers had assembled to pay a last tribute to one who was beloved by all. A short service was conducted at the house by the Rev. 11. Ogden and at the graveside the Rev H V Pigot officiated. The chief mourners were: D Ellison, J Ellison (sons), B Few, W- H Ford, F A Jenkins (son-in-laws), A Few, A C Ellison (grandsons), T Ellison, W Ellison, J Chester, Chester, J Mather (nephews), Mrs D Ellison, Mrs J Ellison (daughters-in- law), Mrs S Jepson (niece), Mrs Watts (Man- chester), Mrs Chadwick, Mrs John Chadwick, Accrington (sisters-in•law), J Massey, .1 II Buckley, J Harrison (Liverpool), J Potter (Crewe), J Johnson (Dewsbury.) WIFE ASSAULT.—Hobert Pembertcn, labourer, 27, Winwick-road. was charged with having assaulted his wife, Sarah Ellen, on Sunday.—The wife stated that her husband came home drunk and, accompanied by three other men,'ltarted kicking his cap about the street. A young woman secured the cep and she (prosecutrix)said she would throw it in the fire. Her husband then butted her with his head, seized her by the hair and dragged her round the room. For protection she seized the poker, which the prisoner took from her, and used it. upon her. He was so violent that no one dared approach hirn.—Prisoner denied lotting his wife, but admitted he 'las so drunk that he did not. know what ho was doing. His wife's parents caused all the bother.—lnspector said he understood the prisoner would not keep his employment.—Mrs Pemberton said her husband bad been "carrying on" for eleven years.— Pemberton was fined 20s. including costs, or in default 14 days. EXPLOSION AT ALKALI WORKS.

At the Muspratt Works, Widnes. of the United Alkali Company on Tuesday afternoon an explosion occurred by which four men were killed and two others injured. The men were engaged in a shed repairing damage to the chlorate of potassium plant suffered during the stoppage caused by the coal strike. A metal pan,known as a doyer, which measured 4ft. by Bft. and weighed seven tons,had been lifted by pcks,liad been repaired,and was being lowered itto position again when the accident happened. A few seconds after the lowering had begun a. blue flame shot out across the shed. There was not a loud report, but the flash and dense fumes pouring from the building soon attracted other workmen to the spot. They attempted an entrance, only to be driven back by tbe choking gases. Hose pipes were brought and water was pumped into the shed. Then when the air had been a little purified the men made their way in. All those who had been at work inside were lying on the groor d, burned and blackened by the flames, which had consumed their clothes Peter Groorne, of Princes-street, whose body had been terribly charred, was so injured that he died as he was carried into the open air. T Abraham, foreman, of Denton-street. died later in the evening at the accident hospital. F Moss, Bower-street, and W Davies, Fern- worth-street, were also very seriously injured and died ilao following day. M Ilealey, Margret-street, and G Marsh, of Havelock- terrace, suffered less.

Rear-Admiral Sir Percy Moreton Scott, inventor of the gun-canisges which enabled naval guns to be used at Ladysmith, was fined 12 for excee‘ling the motor speed-limit. Defendant admitted the offence. MISS GORNALLS CONCERT.

The self-sacrificing labours of the Bold-street Cripples' Guild received well-merited encouragement on Tuesday evening when a concert, promoted by Miss Mary Gornall, in aid of the funds of the organization, was held in Bold-street Schoolroom. It roust have been gratifying to all concerned to see such a repre- sentative audience and a kindly thoughtfulness had prompted the extension of an invitation to the cripples themselves, many of whom were present and greatly appreciated the music. Miss Gornall had enlisted the services of a body of vocalists and instrumentalists who are well known in the district, though some of them are new to the local concert platform, and the programme presented numerous interesting features Mrs. A If. Crosfield's reputation as a pianist is sufficiently high ti focus pleasur- able anticipation in a special degree on the items for which she was responsible. In her performances of Preludes No. 1-3 and Scherzo No. 1 (Chopin) and " Carnaval " (Schumann) she displayed that brilliance of technique and true artistic insight which make her playing a delight to listen to. The Schumann composition, with its wonderful wealth of tone colour and variety of movement, was so perfectly given that an encore tvaa accorded and Mrs. Crosfield responded with a delightful rendering, full of taste and poetic temperament, of Chopin's Vale in E. Minor. Miss Evelyn M. Norton, who makes artistic use of a rich mezzo-soprano voice, invested with great depth of feeling her songs How deep the slumber of the Hoods (Lowe) "Summer Roses ' (Dunkels) and "One of us two " (S.hapiro.) Her enunciation was particularly good. Mrs. B. S. Townroc figured with success both as vocalist and elocutionist. She sang "La Serenata " (Braga) with sympathy and expres- sion but the effect would have been considerably improved if the violin obligate and pianoforte accom- paniment had been more subdued. Mrs Townroe's recitations were most effectively given, the moving appeal of. "The Glory of the Garden" and the tender, quaint simplicity of " Yawcob Strauss" being finely realized. Mrs. 11. D. Llos d, the contralto soloist, esslyed an exacting task in Liddle's "Abide with me," and her rendering, while evidencing a pleasing quality of voice, was s )mewhat deficient in pithos and intensity. She was heard to better advantage in "A summer night" (Coring Thomas). The Rev Horace Gray possesses a robust bass voice and created a favourable impression in "The pipes of Pan " (Elgar), which, after a little undue restraint in the opening passages, he sang in a natural Net appealing way. His remaining items, "Under the Greenwood Tree" (Parry) and "Down Chelsea way" (Coates), were given in bright and spirited style. Miss Gladys S. Stewart played several violoncello solos with excellent taste and finish, though the tone was not always irreproachable. It was announced that Miss Stewart had sustained a slight accident ar.d consequently the movement from the Mendelsohn Sonata, which she and Mrs. Crostield should have played, was omitted from the programme. The MisQes Stewart and Gornall gave several vocal duets. The duties of accompanists were carably shared by Miss Gornall and Miss Marjorie Reynolds.

"I AM VERY GRATEFUL FOR THE FREE PRESCRIPTION."

THERE IS NOTHING LIKE IT FOR PAIN.

" It was a lucky day for me uhen I saw in your columns the free prescription that cured dr. Williams. At the time I was down with influenza, and aching in every bone in my body. One doctor said the pains were Neuralgia, while at the hospital they told me they were Neuritis. Takirg medicine was agony to me as it so affected my heart. I had the prescription filled the same night, and in the morning I was up, my cold was gone, my heart normal, and though I bad a little pain during the day, I have not had it since. I gave it to my mate, and ha tells me that he has lost his Rheumatism and Lumbago. " The prescription was this: Go to the nearest chemist's shop and get 60 grains of Kephaldol in tablet form. Take two tablets at once, and then one every hour until the pain is gone. I teok three when went to bed, and broke up my cold."—Mr. Johnson Hicks, Ne 7 castle.

The Examiner, Saturday, April, 27, 1-412.

LIBERAL WOMEN.

SUCCESSFdL WARD SOCIALS.

SPEECHES BY

MR. AND MRS. A. H. CROSFIELD.

The members of the Warrington Women's Liberal Association have every reason to be proud of the wonderful progress of their organization which now occupies a leading place among the women's progres- sive agencies of the country. The enthusiasm displayed is as gtat Hying as the continued increase in member- ship, and leaves no room for doubt as to the usefultif(ss of the part which the association will play in winning back Warrington to its allegiance to the Liberal cause. Two highly successful ward socials have been held this week and splendid receptions have been accorded to Mr. and Mrs. A. H. Cr osfield, who hate spoken at each gathering. The first meeting was for the members in Whitecross Ward and took place in Thewlis-street schoolrocia on Monday evening. The Tramways Committee considered the alternative plan of trackless trams which were in use in Leeds and Bradford and were cheaper than the track system. As the Board of Trade bad no power to sanction such a scheme, it would probably be necessary to promote a Bill in Parliament. largely intermediate causes, being• themselves often caused by foul air, poor food, and wretched homes with dismal surroundings. In many large towns the insurance companies consider certain streets so detrimental to life that they refuse to accept insurances from persons living in them. In 1886 there were 167 streets in the City of Liverpool so banned. The death-rate for England and Wales has been shown to be 14 7. By dividing 1,000 by 14 7 and the quotient by 2, the average expectation of life is shown to be 31. If, however, by the ‘bolition of slums, the death-rate could be reduced to 12 per 1,000, the average expectation of life would go up to 42. We find, therefore, that the slums of England and Wales, by this shortening of human life, cause its 35 million people to die, upon The average, 8 years before their time. Harrogate, a fashionable watering place in Yorkshire, with a death-rate of 106 per 1,000, can expect its lucky inhabitants to live to an average of 47, whilst its neighbour Dewsbury, with its close quarters and unhealthy occupations, has a death-late of 101, and cannot expect its people to live longer than an average of 26 years. At Ebbw Vale the average life-rate will not exceed 22 years, but at the town of Erith the death-rate is so low that the life-rate should be 60 years. Some years ago Dr. Drysdale estimated the average age at death of the nobility, gentry, and professional classes in England and I Wales to be 55 years ; while at the same time, among 'artisan classes at Lambeth it was only 29, a shorten- ing of life through financial position of 26 years. I Tuberculosis is the white man's plague. Dr. George Newman, the Chief Medical Officer to the Board of Educatien, says that it slays every year in England and Wales, more than 50,000, and he adduces the following facts : Finsbury has been subdivided into 70 small areas,and the high mortality areas are the poorest. They yield 10 deaths per 1,000 more than the average of the Borough. It is the chief cause of premature adult mortality, and has for the past five nears been the cause of 10 per cent, of all the deaths which have occurred in Finsbury. But amongst the poorest classe of casual worker it has been the cause of 50 per cent. of the deaths. The death-rate in 1906 for the whole of Finsbury was 201 per 1,000. but amongst the very poor inhabiting the one-room tenements, it was 39 per l,000: It is impossible to grasp the full significance of these figures. Of course, they show an excess death- rate of 27 per 1,000 over healthy places, but it is difficult to realise what that difference means. It means that one in every 37 is marked for death each year because of the locality in which his lot is cast, and from which locality he has but little chance of escape. Referring to the mortality from this disease in some country districts the Registrar-General reports" Therein no doubt that this is due in pelt to the return to their homes of young adults who have migrated to the towns in Reich of more lucrative employment, and who had contracted tbe disease there. Thus in his opinion the conditions of the town labourers' life are partly responsible for even rural death-rates.

(Alderman Bennett) will bn delighted to furnish full particulars to any who are interested. We have pielsure in reprinting the following verses by Alderman 13enneLt which appear in " Garden Cities and Town Planning

THE CALL OF THE COUNTRY. (A Plea for the Garden Suburbs Movement.)

WHEN you may dwell in a garden, why should you live

in every five dies in its first year. There are 27,000 I infant deaths in these million homes of slumdoin, of whom only 10,800 would have died had they been born in happier conditions. This is a deliberate wasting of 16,200 young lives annually thrmgh England's folly in allowing them to be born in slums. The mortality of country and town born infanta is almost the same during the first three months of life, thus showing how equal are the chances of life with which children start. After the first three months, to the twelfth, there are three infantile deaths in towns to every two in the country, showing the influence of environment during these early months. These figures do not by any means give the total cost of the diseases and deaths that are caused by olumdom. What are the Infirmary, Hospital, Work- house, and Asylum expenses that are indirectly caused by the slums? In London, only three out of every five persons who die breathe their last among their relatives at home, the other two out of the five die in hospital, workhouse, asylum, or prison. In the country generally, one person, out of every five who die, passes away in one of these institutions. These total 100,000 a year who die away from home and cared for by strangers. To care for these and for the sick either the ratepayers or the benevolent people of England and Wales are maintaining 2,902 Poor-law Infirmaries, Hospitals, and Asylums. These represent a probable expenditure of say £10,000,000 a year, at least half of which is incurred through poverty and its attendant evils. And it must be remembered that in addition to those thing in these institutions there are many hundreds of thousands of sick people who are cared for annually by Infirmary, Hospital or Con- valescent Home. These thousands who have been knocked down by the slums are propped up by the loving care of doctors and nurses and then sent back to be knocked down again by reeking court or beastly den. Half the diseases as well as half the deaths would be avoided if the poor of our nation were able to live in healthy homes. During 1908 the number of deaths per thousand from scarlet fever and diphtheria was exactly four times as great in Bethnal Green and Bermondsey as in Hampstead. Three out of fcur of those dying from these diseases in these poor neigh- bourhoods need not have died if they bad been able to live in healthy Hampstead. The last question is the most serious of all. What is the loss to the country, not on the 16,200 who die in their first year amid the slums, but on the balance of the 135,0001wh0 do not die in their first year but who possibly for many years are a burden and a drag upon their fellow men ? If slum conditions were fatal to all those born in them, the loss would be terrible, but the more terrible loss is caused by those who survive their earlier years, but never escape from the influence of their birth-place upon their physical, mental, and moral natures. By the time 21 years of age is reached, the numbers are probably reduced by half, but, if so, this leavdi 67,500 grown men and women who through their lives Will carry the sinister bar of their slum origin. Every farmer knows that to get robust horses, the foals during their first year must have fresh air, or the whole future of their lives is irretrievably damaged. Valuing men as you , would horses, the difference in hiring value between a well-bred, healthy and robust human animal, as compared with a sluggish, languid, slum-reared human animal is at the very least 10s. per week. In thirty years' service this is a loss to the community of £730 upon each of these slum reared images of Divinity. " Fashioned and shaped by DO will of their own. And helplessly into life's history thrown ; Born by the law that compels men to be Born to conditions they cannot foresee." Who is to blame? Rather let it be asked, who is to blame if this present waste of human life is allowed 'to co'ntinuo ? Maaterr, who did sin, this man or his

_ in a'sluin Why should you toil in the sweltering town when the birds are calling, "Como " ? When the grass is green in the meadows and the daisies golden-eyed, Come, for the birds a^e calling ; come to the country side !

pad-ent4 that be was born "—in the slums? "Neither bath this man sinned nor his parents, but-- - ? " The answer must be left to the conscience of the nation. From "Progress."

It is difficult to put a cash value upon human life, yet if an Australian sheep farmer lost 97,460 sheep in one year by avoidable causes, he would soon reckon up the value of the sheep which be had lost. Tile loss of 97,460 lives is, however, only a part of the cost to the community entailed through the possession of slums. As Dr. Newsholme says," From an economical point of view sickness is more important than death." Charles Dickens put the case in a nutshell when he said : " It concerns a man more to know the risks of the fifty illnesses that may throw him on his back than the possible date of the one death that must come. We must have a list of the killed and of the wounded too." This is, of course, difficult to obtain, but we might take it that there are two years lost from work by illness for every death that actually occurs. The .Friendly Societies place this at a higher figure, so this is a moderate estimate. The 97,460 deaths multiplied by two gives us 184,920 as the number of persona ill .through the year in consequence of bad housing or insanitary conditions. At least 35,000 of these would be men, and we might reckon £6O a year each as their loss of wages, giving us a total of over .f. 2,000,000 loss to the workers' homes through father being off work. This is leaving out of account all questions of ordinary illness or drunken- ness, merely those whose homes or whose employment was so injurious as to cause them to he away from work. In London during 1906 there were 36,800 cases notified of scarlet fever, diptheria, enteric and erysipelas, and these diseases generally originate in the homes of the poorest. However had the slums may be for the men, who pnly come there to sleep, they are worse for the women who have to be in them all day long. The effect of the slums on the wives and mothers is still more difficult to reckon. They may not complain, they may not even know why they are languid and anwmic, and they possibly take to drink to temporarily deaden their craving for brighter surroundings. If only 2s. 6d. per woman per week be allowed for this loss of energy and for the consequent visits to the chemist, the 'doctor, or the publican, we have £6,000,000 a year being wasted, as well as one million mothers incapacitated for the proper discharge of their duties. In 1908 there were 942,611 births in this country, and at least 135,000 of these took place in these one million unhealthy homes. In healthy surroundings the infantile deathrate is often not more than one in fifteen births, but in eluniticnn at lenet one chilli

Come from the cro Rded alleys where you scarce can see the sky For the shade of the tall brick buildings,and the smoke- clouds rolling by ; Come from the .qualid pavements and the courts where the children pine ; Come from the haunts of the fever to the fields where the cowslips shine! Come, for the cuckoo calls you ; come where the blue- bells grow ; Where the happy hedges whiten with the hawthorn's nerved mow ; Come, where the brooklet gurgles ; come where the c ,rocrake cries ; Come to the woodbine hedges under the sunlit skies ! 0 friends, will ye hug your fetters when we seek to set you free ? Would you rather have the slimy kerb than the throstle's minstrelsy ? Is the sight of your ash-tubs sweeter than the smell of the new-mown hay ? Is the gutter the place for a flower-faced child in our English land to play ? Are you lasppy under your chimneys, amid the reek and the roar ? Would you rather have the flaming forge than the roses at your door? Is the filthy pavement dearer than the daisy-spangled sod, And the breath of your back-yards sweeter than the unveiled face of God? We will build you homes to dwell in, and places wherein to play; You shall delve in your fruitful gardens at the close of the busy day; You shalt twine the blue clematis round your windows and your walls; And your rest shill be sweet, in the summer heat, when the purple twilight falls. And here you shall bring up children whose faces are fair to see, Whose limbs are strong and whose hearts are glad with a new-found liberty; And the filth and the smoke and the squalor shall slot% ly pass away, And the night grow bright in the rosy light and the peace of the dawning day! ARTHUR BENNETT.

GARDEN SUBURBS.

PROGRESS OF THE MOVEMENT IN WARRINGTON.

In connection with the local celebrations of the National Health Week, we understand that the War- rington Garden Suburbs Ltd., are arranging to conduct parties over their estates at Great Sankey and Grappenhall on Saturday next. We need scarcely !point out that the Garden Suburbs movement has a ' very direct and intimate bearing on the all-important question of the pub:ic health. In every garden suburb which has been started in this country the health conditions are exceedingly good and the promoters of the first garden city—Letchworth —claim to have the lowest death rate in England. This will be readily understood when it is borne in mind that one of the fundamental conditions of all these estates is that the number of houses shall be strictly limited, the average 'being about 12 to the acre, and that there shall be an ample supply of recreation grounds and open spaces. The Warrington company, which was founded about four years ago, has acttd on these principles and in the plans of its estates, which are most attractively arranged, both these leading points have received careful attention. The Great Sankey estate is about 20 acres in extent and is very pleasantly situated. A pretty park of about three acres has already been partially laid out and smaller open opaces are also provided for. Twenty houses have been erected at varying rents and they are all let, whilst it is intended to build more houses in the immediate future. On the Grappenhall estate provision is made for a large park and recreation ground. There are several smaller open spaces and a portion of the land is set apart for allotments. This estate has also an inviting rural environment and contains some fine trees. Eight houses have been erected and four additional ones have just been condmeneed. Here also they seem to be in good demand, and the com- pany are prepared to lease attractive plots of land to builders and investors or intending residents at very low. rates. This they can afford' to do, as the ultimate dividend payable to the shareholders is strictly limited to 5 percent. Two co-operative tenants societies have been registered to assist in the develop. ment of the property —the Warrington Tenants, Ltd., and the Grappenhall Tenants, Ltd.--and these are being worked on the lines which have proved so eminently successful at Ealing, Hampstead and numerous other places. The tenant members partici- pate in any profits that may be earned beyond 5 per cent., and enjoy the privilege of managing their own property. The movement has made strenuous strides in other parts of the country, but Warrington has been a little slow in taking up the idea of co-operative ownership We do not doubt that, as its merits become better understood the scheme will make sub- stantial headway. It should especially appeal to supporters of the co-operative movement. It is difficult to over-estimate the advantage of living in well-built property amid such agreeable surroundings, and with the certainty that the speculative builder will not be permitted to crowd too many houses together, or to destroy the amenities of the estates. Mr. Charles E. Parker, the chairman of the present •company, has taken the greatest possible interest in thn moveuxernt frdni the first, arid he or the ilecretary

INFANT MORTALITY.

What Germany is Doing.

In ING the death rate of infants in their first year was : - . .

..„ . St. John's Warrington. Ward. Norway 13.-nniark 11.2 1906 151 21,1 France l3'B 1910 11•3 11•1

Letchworth Garden City ...,

Well done St. John's Ward The backward state of Germany in regard to housing conditions and infant morality has forced the nation to face these problems and it is doing so in a thorough fashion. It was only in 1905 that the subject of infant morality was seriously investigated. These investiga- tions brought to light the very low percentage of breastfed children and the high percentage of illegiti- mate births. It was in 1905 that "schools for mothers" as we call them, or " Infant care stations," as named by Germans, were started. These schools are not, like our own, left to the precarious support of private enterprise, but are mainly municipal institutions, equipped, staffed and maintained by city funds Germany during the last six years has organised 251 of these schools, situated in 165 different towns ; tbey are not entirely municipal, but mainly so. In Berlin there are se • en such "schools," each with a senior physician, several assistants and a sister in charge. and three or four trained nurses. Any mother whose family's income is Jess than 30s. a week may bring her baby and receive medical advice. In 1909 the total number of bqbies brought WBS 13,194, and the number of consulta- tions 156,510, and visits paid to the homes 38,266. When the schools were started in 1905 only 399 pecent. were breastfed, in 1910 the percentage stood at 651, while the infant death-rate of the city during the same period was reduced from 20 6to 15.6, against Warrington 151 to 11-3 During the first year that the children were brought to the consultations the death-rate among 'those so brought was 8.4, last year it had dropped to 4 per cent. In Berlin in various capacities there are 127 doctors engaged to attend to the health of the school children. _

In vital statistics this country shows that we are still a good way ahead of Germany, but the Germans appear to be tackling the problems more energetically than we are j anti are ntit afraid of gpc'n'ding ntittur, litatilic and

ORDER OF DRUIDS.

SUCCESSFUL SOCIAL AND INTERESTING PRESENTATIONS.

A pleasant social at which two interesting presenta- tions were rude, was held hbder the auspices of the Druids Camp Lodge (N0.15(3). of the An,iont Orier of Druids in the Crown and Sceptre 11-tel on Monday evening. D.G.M. Bro T Derb‘ shire presided slid was supported by Bros W Bibby D.D.G M. (Jun ), E Allen D.C.S., R Perdue D.T., F W smith N.G. A , S Sirncock V.C.A., William Bibby P.D.G M. (seri), S.‘dney Packer P.D.G.111. secretary, Henry Lesion P.N.G.A. and P.D.G. M.S. J Ijibbu, G ltushtm, J Jacsson, E Po3nton, S Hankey, W Ham key and James Ray. The CHAIRMAN said they were met to hi nour two of their w,rthy members, Bro. Packer (their secretary) and P.N.G.A. Bro. Hodnet. He thought they would all agree with him when be said that they all had at bola the welfare of the Lodge and of Druidism gener- ally. (Hear, hear.) They could not forget the great catastrophe that had happened in the wreck of the "Titanic." It was an appalling disaster, and he hoped they would feel with him and express their sympathy with all concerned in that wreck. (Hear, hear.) It had come as a great blow to all the civilised world. (Hear, hear.).

'the CHAIRMAN then proposed the toast of "The King and Queen," which was duly honoured. Bro. E. ALLEN, D.C.S., in proposing "The Order of Druids" said they were all well aware that in the near future they would be uuder different conditions. The National Insurance Act would come into operation in s very short time and it would make their work all the more arduous and of a more particular kind than in the past. They would be supervised by Government. officials—only the mention of Government officials to some people would make them rather nervous, and possibly when some people become nervous the work would not be carried on as well as if that nervousness had not spread. He had been thinking of what his position would be under the Act and had come to the conclusion that it would be far better that anything in connection with the Act should be done by the officials of the lodges. Friendly Society secretaries and officials had not been adequately paid in the past and the Government were not going to do much more for them in the tuture. If the duplication of work had to be carried on they would not, have snfficient money to pay the lodge officials. The Central Office would have the first pull on the funds and when Ihe district had had its pull there would be very little left for the lodges when their turn came. The lodge officials should be paid because they would have most of the work and would have to bear the blame. (Applause) tiro W BIBBY, in responding to the toast, said their chief business that night was to honour two of their worthy workers. He did not think there was a more conscientious and more bard working secretary than Bro Packer in the whole Order of Druids. (Applause.) They were fortunate in having their corresponding secretary on the Board of Management for he could give them the last word on the Insurance Act. In their society they bad adopted the principle of equalisation which gave the oldest members the full benefits to which they e ere entitled. (Applause). They could not see how it was going to be affected under the Insurance Act. They bad been successful in Warrington with their investments and the interest earning capacity of that money was as good as any other society in W ,rrington received. They had decided to have a re-valuation of those investments end they would know just how they stood within the coming week. Applause). In making the presentation Bro S Efancee said be bad not known anyone to work harder for the good of the Order than Bro Packer. He had gone through every office in the lodge and he (the speaker) had great pleasure in presenting him with a gold signet ring and a purse of gold which he hoped would show their appreciation of his valuable services. In regard to Bro Hodnet, he had brought many members into the society and had worked hard for the cause. (Hear, hear.) He presented him with a silver medallion of the Order. (Applause.) Bro W BIBBY, jun., said he felt be ought not to allow that opportunity to pas without adding his testimony. Bro Packer had been an excellent secretary. It bad been mentioned that equslisation paved the way to success but they could not have been successful without Bro Packer at the helm. (Hear, hear.) He bad saved the Druids Camp Lodge which was now one of the most prosperous in Warriogton. Bro Hodnet had been the means of introducing a large number of members. He was certainly one of the pioneers who opeued the new lodge at Winwick. He hoped that lodge would prosper as much as the Druids Camp Lodge had done. Wear hear.) Bro. PACKER, in reply, said whatever duty he bad done he had performed it to the best of his ability. He thanked the outside brethren who had come that night to testify to his ability and also the district officers who had been his mainstay all alone the line. He could not find suitable words to express his appreciation of their kindness. He felt proud of the Order and proud of tbe members. He hoped prosperity would be the reward of all their efforts. (Applause). Bro. HODNET said so far as King George Lodge, the new lodge at Winwiok, was concerned they bad over 100 members up to the present. (Applause). Bro ALLEN added his tribute to the worth of the two recipients. . .

Other 'toasts honoured were "The Warrington Die. trict," proposed by Bro R Perdue, D.T., and responded to by Bro. W. Bibby (jurr.) " Druids Camp Lodge," proposed by Bro G Rushton; and responded to by Bro 11 Loaton, P.W.G.A.

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

REV. A. M. MITCHELL AND "THE TRADE."

Opposition to the Chairman.

Some unusual speeches and objections marked the election of chairman at the annual meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians which was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday. There were present: Mrs Collingwood, Mrs Simpson, Miss Broadbent, Beim T R Matthews and A M Mitchell, Dr Murray, Messrs G Mather, A .1 Jolley, J Billinge, A Brooks' W Houghton, W C Brooks, W Eurne3s, It Lee, J A Hughes, J A Rushworth, J W Knowles, W Flanagan, M W Seton, J Cook, T Ritchie, A Bottomley (clerk), II W Peacock (deputy clerk), 0 Moss (assistant master) and A Wright (superintendent of Padgate Cottage Homes). ELECTION OF CHAIRMAN.

REV. A. M. MITCHELL AND " TIIE TRADE." The CLERK having been elected to the chair pro tern, Mrs. COLLINGWOOD proposed that Mr Mather, who has already been chairman for two years, should occupy the position for another year. MISS BROADBENT seconded. Mr. BILLING E proposed the election of Mr E L Bent (vice-chairman), who had, be said, for many years held an important position on the Newton Urban District and was very highly respected by his own townspeople. Honours and favours came his way without solicitation. As chairman he would be a credit to the Board. Mr. HEGIIES seconded and Mr. LEE supported. Mr. IloconToN said Mr Bent had done his duty most assiduously. The Rev. A. M. MITCHELL said he was very sorry he was not able to join in the chorus of praise of the ex-vice-chairman (Mr Bent). He was disappointed that Mr 13illinge bad spoken in the way he had. He (the speaker) thought the proposal which emanated from Mrs. Collingwood was much more desirable. He took exception to Mr Bent as chairmen for ore reason which would be known to everybody present—he objected to a licensed victualler holding the position. Mr. Hue; itEs : I think Mr. Mitchell is out of omen The Rev A. M. MITCHELL : lam not out of order. lam speaking quits to tne point Continuing, he said the same argument applied to the Nice-chairmanship.' Bo he did not quito know how it come to pa,s that Mr Bent orsN appointed to that position a few years ago. For his own pert he (the speaker) opposed the nomitation of anyone who was engaged in the business or occupation of a publicaa. He knew perfectly well that Mr Best was a man of large business ; he was a farmer and a grocer but the fact remained that he wa sengaged in the liquor traffic and having regard to the fact that so much of the Poverty and disease of the country was due to the curse of drink, it was unseemly thatanyone who was engaged in that traffic should preside over the deliberations of the Board. Ile asked Mr. Bent if he would be kind enough to withdraw his name. The Board was a little uncomplimentary in not nominating one of the ladies to the chair. It would have been a pleasant change. All the ladies were so capable that it would be difficult to say who was the roost capaule but if any member would propose one of the ladies he would be very happy to second it. Mr RusHavoßTH : This is out of order, Mr Chair- man. the Rev. A. M. MITCHELL : As the matter stands, I will be prepared to support Mrs Collingwood's motion. Mr. FURNESS said he had not intended to take any part in the debate but one could not sit quietly and listen to the reasons given by the roe, gentlemen why Mr Bent should not be elected chairman. It came with very bad grace from Mr Mitcheli—(bear,jacer)—because it shower" an amount of intolerance that was not com- mendable in a man who was supposed to exercise free thought and action. Ile (the speaker) was broad enough in his views to consider that every man on that Board, whatever his profession or calling might be, was quite equal to any otner man, and that was the principle he had always tried to maintain. The Rev AM MITCHELL : Is this quite in order ? Mr. FURNESS said he had always taken that, line in the allocation of positions on that Board. Mr. Mather had occupied the chairmanship for two years. He (the speaker) was not in a position to ray what Mr Mather's intentions might be, but he did not think he would be wise to allow his name to stand for a third year. The honours—if they were honours at all—should go round as frequently as possible. He supported the nomination of Mr Bent because he considered he would do justice to the Board, credit to himself, and be an ornament to the position. 'hoe ttev. A. M. MITCHELL: What about the ladies? Miss BROADBENT said she strongly objected to Mr. Mitchell's remarks, and to his aspersiots on Mr. Bent. She had always thought it was very important that licensed victuallers should be men of high character, as they had great influence in their own sphere. Mr. KEow Las said ha felt sorry that Mr Mitchell should come there to show his—should he say peculiarities? Mr. FURNESS : Idiosyncracies. (Laughter). Mr. KNowr.}:s : Yes. They both mean about the same, but I did not like using a strong word because I had not quite looked it up. (Laughter). The Rev. A. M. MITCHELL: It is no pleasure to me to do this. Mr. KNOWLES said one felt sorry that Mr Mitchell should be so rude and tin-Christian as to make such a personal attack on a brother man. They were all present for the same purpose—the care of the poor and the proper administration of relief. To see a house falling out" among itself was painful. One of the first teachings of Christianity ought to be forbearance, the showing of a good and hopeful feeling and living at peace one with another. Something must have " got at" Mr Mitchell that morning. (Laughter.) His vegetables must have gone wrong and put him in a liverish state. (Laughter.) He (the speaker) hoped Mr Bent would not say a word in reply to Mr Mitchell. It would be the finest way to treat such an attack. At the same time he (the speaker) might say from his own knowledge of the licensing trade that the qualifications for a publican at the present day were more strict even than for admission to the right to preach the Gospel. A publican's character was sifted from his youth and his capabilities in every respect were inquired into. They even wanted to know his ancestors. (Laughter). Mrs SIMPSoN : I don't think this is in order..

Mr IINOWLES : It may not be in order but it is in reply to what Mr Mitchell has said. Mr MATITER thanked the ladies who had so kindly proposed his re-election to the chair, lie had occupied the position for two years and had very pleasant recol- lections of that time, but he would not like to oppose Mr Bent and asked the ladies i ".> withdraw his name. I,.Tr Mather's name was then withdrawn. Ott Mr Bent's name being put to the meeting, his election was approved, Mr Mitchell being the only one to express dissent. On a formal vote the CLERK announced that 16 Guardians had Toted for Mr Bent ; 2 against, and 3 had abstained from voting. Mr. BENT, on taking the chair, said he did not wish to refer to what had taken place that morning. They knew the position that certain people took up. No doubt the coming year would be a very eventful one. There was a lot of work before them and he was nut very anxious to take the position for the reason that they had several schemes in band with which Mr. Mather was more closely acquainted.

A NOBLE SAVAGE. A TRUE STORY.

A Scotch Highl-arieerWa-s—i-aken prisoner by a tribe of Indians, in the middle of the eighteenth century. His life was about to be sacrificed, when the chief adopted him as his son. They carried him into the interior; he learnt their language, assumed their habits, and became skilful in the use of their arms. After a season, the same tribe began their route to join the French army, at that time opposed to the English. It was necessary to ' pass near to the English lines during the night. Very .early in the morning, and it was spring, the old chief roused the young Highlander from his repose; he took him to an eminence, and pointed out to him the tents of his countrymen. The old man appeared to be dreadfully agitated and there was a keen restlessness in his eye. After a pause: " I lost," said he, "my ,only son in the battle with your nation; art you the only son of your father? and do you think that your father is yet alive? " The young man replied, "I am the only son of my father, and I hope that my father is yet alive." They stood close to a beautiful mignolia in full blossom. The prospect was

THE CLOCK. He stands in a corner from morning till night, A patient old thing with no feet; His face is as solemn and round as a moon, And, oh! so exceedingly neat. From breakfast to supper, Bight through the day, "Tick-took, tick-took, 1 am only the clock, Tick-tcck, tick-took," he'll say. his bands are quite tidy; they grow on his face. When I grow to be big I shall know Why one is so long and the other so short, And one he moves fast, and one slow. From breakfast to supper, Right on through the day, "Tick-took, tick-tock, I am only. the clock, Tick-took, tick-took," he'll say. At night, when I'm sleeping, he keeps wide awake To see what the little mice do; He watches the brownie creep in through the blind, His little red shoes wet with dew. Vrorn night-time to daytime, Right on through the day, " Tick-took, tick-tock, I am only the clock, Tick-tock, tick-tock," he'll say. And when it comes morning I wish he would tell— I ask him, but never a traco Of the wonderful things which be saw in the night Does he show in his sober old face. From breakfast to supper, Right on through the day, " Tick-tock, tick-tock, I am only the clock, Tick-tock, tick-took," he'll say.

CHARGE AGAINST FARMER'S SON. Shooting A—flair at Cuerdley.

A sensation was created in the Widnes distrct on ' Sunday by a shooting affair which occurred at Cuerdley Hall Farm early in the morning. It is alleged that four men, residents of Moss Bank, Widnes, entered the farm premises for the purpose of stealing potatoes. William Ilarrison, son of the owner owing to previous raids went with dog and gun, and at two o'clock he saw men in the potato pit in the stack- yard. The gun, it is stated, accidentally went off, and later Edward Shannon and William Griffin were found lying on tho ground injured. With police assistance they were conveyed to the Widnes Hospital, where Shannon was found suffering from gunshot wounds in the left arm. Griffin was more seriously wounded in the left side of the cheat and abdomen. At the Warrington Occasional Court on Monday, William Harrison, the son of a farmer, of Cuerdley Hall Farm, was charged with shooting at two men, named William Griffin and Edward Shannon, of Moss Bank, Widnes. Superintendent Strickland said that Mr. Harrisomthe father of the prisoner, had for some time suspected people of stealing his potatoes from a hog in the stock- yard. About two o'clock on Sunday morning the prisoner got up and left the farm with the intention of looking round the yard. lie took with him a double- barrelled gun, which was loaded. On approaching the stackyard he saw four men stealing potatoes from the hog. The gun was fired, and the men received wounds in the body. The prisoner's explanation of the firing was that he was carrying the gun under his arm with his hand near the trigger, when by some moans it went off. The gun, it appears, was fired on two occasions. Two of the men, Uritlin and Shannon were so seriously injured that the prisoner conveyed them b a trap to Widnes Police Station, where he made a state- ment to Sergeant Moseley. Afterwards they were taken to the Widnes Accident Hospital and detained, Dr. Hutchinson attended to their injuries, and extracted seventeen pellets from the abdomen of Griffin and thirty-one out of the arms and body of Sitiannon. The other two men, Anthony Griffin and John Griffin, made good their escape, but they also received a number of pallet wounds. Dr Hutchinson had informed the police that there was no immediate danger respecting the two men who were in the hospital. A remand for a week was necessary in order that the police might complete their inquiries. Mr J LY Knowles, solicitor who defended, had no objection to the application, but applied for bail, which was allowed in two sureties of ,ClOO each.

CHESHIRE EDUCATION RATE.

A DECREASE OF A HALFPENNY.

Cheshire's budget for education in the county was presented by Mr It T Richardson at a meeting of the Education Committee at Crewe on Monday, Dr W Hodgson presiding. Dealing with higher education, Mr Richardson pointed out that the estimated expendi- ture was £91,707 and the income £86.492, leaving a deficit of £28,214, as against £28,179 last year. A rate of 2d. in the pound produced on the new assess- ment £31,940, as against £30,746 last year. By the 2d rate they were fairly well covered. For elementary education they estimated that a sum of £239,3:32 would be required, which was an increase of £2,700 on last year. The income from grants would amount to £127,222, which was an increase of .£2,253 on last year. The amount required from the rates would be £112,110, egainst £11L663 last year. Last year they levied a lid rate, which produced .C 121,517. This year he thought a 10.!,d rate would meet their requirements. This was TIIE FIRST DF.CRY:NSE TREY IINI) EVER. SROWN. They began with a 31c1 rate, then 6d., then Bd.. 3c1,, lOd. and lid. The fact that they were now beginning to go back again showed that a check was being put on the enormous education expenditure in the county. He believed that the decrease would be maintained. The county had been threatened with a scarcity of teachers, and it bad been suggested that if they paid them higher wages they would get more teachers. He did aot think that that was the reason for the diminution in the number. There must be something else that made the profession not so attractive as it ought to be. Since the Committee took over the teachers there had been an increase in their salaries of £52,000, which was more than a halfpenny rate. In face of that it could hardly be said that it was money that was keeping others back The estimates were adopted.

Cheshire Teachers Want More Powers. The question of corporal punishment in Cheshire schools provoked another discussion at the County Education Committee at Crewe on Monday. Mr. J. ilinchcliff (the teachers' representative on the committee) produced a petition, signed by 800 school teachers in the county, protesting against the modifica- tions in the corporal punishment regulations recently made by the Cheshire Education Authority. The petition declared that if the regnlations are enforced, the discipline of many classes, and sometimes of whole schools, will t 5 ruined. They therefore requested that the recently-adopted regulation —viz., that corporal punishment shall be administered by the head teacher only, with few exceptions—may be rescinded, so that the regulation shall reed : "Corporal punishment shall be administered only by the head teacher, or by fully certified assistant teachers after three years experience who may have had such power delegated to them by the head teacher, with the aasent of the managers." After discussion, this subject was adjetlrned for con- sideration at a special meeting,

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Survivors.—Professor T. Claire, writing in the St. Bartholomew's Hospital Journal, says that women can tolerate pain and privation better than men, and that their capacity for enduring cold and exposure is , the greater. This is contrary to the generally accepted , view respecting the psychology of men and women, but we remember that whou the Delhi was wrecked, the late Duke of Fife suffered more from the after effects than did the Princess Royal or her daughters. It is not, bow- ever,surprising that a large number of the women saved from the Titanic, should have arrived at Now York in a state of prostration. People who have survived great calamities have related that their worst sufferinga have come with the reaction, follow- ing the numbness and exhaustion, and it it difficult to imagine a more terrible ordeal than that which the survivors of the Titanic had to go through. They were alert hours in the boats before the arrival of the rescuing ship in darkness and penetiating cold, and for many of the women, far worse than physical sufferinge, was the mental torture that they had left husbands or dear Ones behind to perish. A score of those newly-widowed by the cruel waters, are under twenty-live, many of them brides of only a few weeks or menthe. Such tragic partings are one of the saddest features of the wrer k, to which almost universal refer- ence has been made in the churches and places of worship. No calamity of recent years has evoked the national sorrow as the Atlantic disaster ha l done, or arm e 1 more widespread sympathy with the bereaved. Some London Exhibitions.—London is prumised various pageants and exhibitions this summer, one of the most interesting being the International ' Horticultural Exhibition in the grounds of Chelsea Hospital, which the King and Queen are to open On May 22nd. There has been no horticultural exhibition in London sioce 1866. It is a form of enterprise we have mostly left to our neighbours on the e tritinent, but this forthcoming exhibition will do something to make amends, and it is to be the greatest floral show end fete ever seen. It will cover over twenty acres of the grounds of the Royal Hospital, one tent alone comprising over three a ;res, and the exhibition will illustrate the wonderful progress which British floriculture has made in the lath generation. It will attract visitors rnd exhibitors from all perts of the world, and will be the moat popular rendezvous for fashionable society during the eight days it is to remain open. At Earl's Court, Mrs. George Cornwallis West is buoy completing arrangements for the opening of htr Shakespeare's England Exhibi(ion, designed to represent London as it was in Tudor times. The White City at tehepherd'a Bush is to be devoted to an Anglo-Latin Exhibition of art and industry. In the snmmer the King and Queen are to open it new dock at Woolwich, an event which will present the special attraction of a Royal progress on the 1 betties, recalling the times when the river was a great thoroughfare for State occasions.

The Inventor of the Sewing Machine.—The sewing machine, which now finds a place in nearly every home, was tbe dream of inventors for a hundred years or more before it became a practical accomplishment. As far back as 1735 Charles F. Weisenthal took out a patent in this country for a mechanical device and he was not the first to cherish the idea of sewing by machinery. ft was not however, until the year 1830, that a sewing machine of any practical use was evolved and its inventor ass Bai thelemy Thimmonier in whose memory the French are arranging a centenary celebra- tion in Lyons. The sewing machine did not escape the prejudice and hostility entertained by the working classes against most forms of machinery, which tended to take the place of hand labour, and this and its many imperfections in it seedy days, prevented it from making rapid headway. Thirnmouier died in 1857, like so many nther inventors—br.)ken-hearted and in abject poverty. flow his invention has become of world-wide utility, is shown by the enormous output of the great sewing machine manufacturers. One firm alone employs 1,200 hands, near Glasgow, has sold twenty million machines sin,:.e its establishment in 1850, and now supplies the world with about two million machines ev.try year,. .

Curious Colour Schemes.—Ths descriptions of some of the colour schemes of the moment are misleading altogether, so crude and bizarre are they when put into print. Yet in appearance they are quite fascinating, if not alluring, The milliners this year are nothing if not daring, and succeed by methods known only to themselves' in producing harmonies from blues and greens that would have been pronounced impossible by forme r generations. Ruskin bids us look at the deep blue of a cloudless sky on a sunshiny spring day, through the green foliage of a tree, in order to appre- ciate fully the beauty of this seemingly unharmonions contrast. Our milliners have followed this advice, to judge from their success in schemes in which these two colours are so delightfully contrasted. Another approved blending is rich brown with blue and rose pink. Purple and cerise, and purple and greenish bronze sound impossible, yet are in reality, chsrming. The Pleatet Skirt.—Like so many fashions and revivals, the pleated skirt owes ite resuscitation to the stage. It was lately worn by a popular actress in a

Parisian play. A member of one of the big Paris firms of dressmakers recognising the possibilities the revival would afford, exploited the idea forthwith and the result has exeeeded his most sanguine anticipations. This skirt will probably lengthen the life of the silhouette and for this reason alone, it should be hailed with delight by every woman who has pissed her first youth. Pleated overdresses will, be worn over pleated skirts, and the effect of the clinging, yet elastic lines, revealing in the case of a combination of two shades— here and there pale tones beneath must be seen to be fully realised. Sometimes the overskirt falls in serpentine folds, from the corsage to the hem' yet the whole is close-fitting. It is not improbable that a new line for skirts will result from this revival. This Season's Wrset.—The selection of a corset is a matter of extreme importance, if the natural and beautiful figure is to be attained, because present styles demand several features, among which are drooping shoulders a large waist, flat low bust, and small hips. The chief characteristic is the low bust which is imperative in the case of the slender and stout alike. The back also must be low, and, strange 98 it may ' sound, a pretty back is improved by the low corset, and even by its absence. A new French corset extends scarcely above the waist line, hut very far below it. It is higher at the back than at the frost, nod slopes from the side towards the centre, where there is scarcely any support above the waist. The fashionable corset, it will he seen, whilst longer than last sea.son, is not as stiff, and the bones are as few as possible. The natural waist does away with the necesslty for rigid corseting around the hips, as the figure in time falls into natural , lines where there is no tightening of the waist. Some New Neckwear.—Tne ostrich feather boa is praying a great success, and so is the necklet nlso of plumage fastened on one side by taffetas bows. Although lighter, the mettle of tulle, or lace, is scarrely warm enough for the chilly days of witty spring. Quite new, is a muslin cambric, limp-rullied collar, shaped v like in front, and wide at the back. Ihe design will appeal to women who have become accustomed to the collarless oorssge, for it is very light, withought the restraint of the high band. The Wedding Veil.—The wedding veil grows longer as time goes on, and now floats over the train, and ends in a point. Moreover it is no longer closely arranged mob-cap fashion, to cover the head entirely, but just simply, and apparently loosely attached to the coiffure, though in a manner that (1 rite relieves the wearer from any dragging influence. beautiful Brussels net, hemmed with lace, is preferred by the fashionable bride, the all lace veil being objected to, on account of its weight, and general tiabecomingness.

Mother SEIGEES SYR

MIL TOM MANN ON THE WORKER. A DISMAL IMPRESSION.

Mr. Tom Mann, speaking on Monday at a demonstra- tion at Barrow, said that the standard of the life of the workingclasses was very little higher than was the case a quarter of a century ago, and many died twenty-five years younger than they ought. This wee due to shame- ful incapacity and the exploitation of labour. There was to-day really a wider gap between workers and employers than was ever the case. Humanity, indeed, was constantly changing with an endeavour to make life easier, but in all this the worker was suffering, and consequently there was poverty in the land that only industrial solidarity would remedy.

Stockton Heath.

BIBLE CLASS CYCLISTS.—In connection with St Thomas's Bible Class B " Club, a pleasant inn to Altrincham took plans on Saturday. Twenty-two members turned out. Altrincham was reached about four o'clock, and the party dismounted for tea. Before returning borne several games were arranged and danc- ing was indulged in. The homeward run was then com- menced and Stockton Heath was reached at eight o'clock. PAROCIIIAL COMMITTEE.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

PEOPLE'S PLEASURES.

PREBENDARY WAREFORD AND DANGERS OF RECREATION.

The Rev. Prebendary Wakeforcl, of Liverpool, speak- ing on "Our People's Pleasures" to a congregation of men and women of St. Mary's, Widnes, on Sunday afternoon,said people must not give wa, to bad passions, because it was like a blind man being led by n mad dog. Thera was at present a great danger of our recreations becoming vicious, and great care should be taken that they did not come to our own towns. The betting in connection with the public sport was desecrating it. The stipendiary of Liverpool had a few weeks ago stated that during his career nine cases out of ten children that came before him for theft had committed the thefts in order to procure money to attend picture shows. But ho (the speaker) did believe the evil stopped there. What they saw at picture shows was not always suggestive, but the motion of the picture gave them a highly delirious and intoxi- cating spirit, which resulted in unbalanced reason. That most prove disastrous to the nation and Empire. He believed that picture shows were demoralising to the younger life of the nation. Public hoardings were also another great evil. By suggestive pictures thereon an attempt was being made to debase the imaginations of the people, and the man that was respcusible for them was a public enemy. They could not allow these evils to go without protest, and it was the duty of every man to endeavour to make the atmosphere of their streets and towns cleaner. Referring to Disestablish- ment, he said : Parliament was going to rob the Church of Wales. Why? A bargsin has been made between different parties of politicians, and Home Rule was to be given to Ireland at the cost of the disestablishment of the Church in Wales. RUNCORN RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL.

The annual meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Council was held at Dutton, on Tuesday. At the com- mencement Mr T Wright, the retiring chairman, Proposed that Mr C E Linaker be appointed as chairman for the coming year. He had been chairman of several in3portribt committees end bad been a most diligent and enthusinst,o worker. (Hear, hear). Mr. Pilling, in seconding, said Mr. Linaker was a worthy miss for the office. _ _

Tbe-propcsition was heartily carried and Mr Linaker in reply said be had been connected with the council for a god numbsr of years and bad taken a keen interest in the work be had been called upon to do. (Hear, hear ) It we the duty of every man who bad the ability and time to take an interest in local govern- ment. En their Council they had never been troubled by political or sectarian vi•ws being introduced. ([fear hear.) He thanked them for the honour they had, conferred upon him. (Applause). Mr. woodA ard proposed Mr T Antwiss as the vice- chairman. Mr Linaker spclnded and the proposition was agreed to.—Mr Antwiss suitably responded.

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The annual meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guar- dians was held at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday. Mr Morris proposed the re-eleation of Mr Spruce as chairman. fie was quite certain he had fulfilled his duties in a satisfactory manner. The prop( salon vr!is carried unanimously. On taking the chair, Mr Spruce thanked the Board for the renewal of their confidence. When be was appointed two years ago he thought it would only have been for a short time, and that Boards of Guardians would soon be dispensed with and their duties allocated to some other authority. They wcra still living, and he trusted their work would he carried on in as amicable a .spirit .as in the past. Sometinu s they were charged with being extravagant but their con- stituents should bear in mind that the Board were not the cause of higher rates but if they would look further afield and look at the elaborate buildings for edu- cational purposes and the cost of maintenance of the roads, they would find sufficient cause for grumbling. The Chairmen then moved that Mr U E Linaker, jun., be elected as vice-chairman for the year. He had done his work very well and when the occasion had arisen he had filled the chair in an able manner. No doubt when he came to I e appointed to the chairmanship he would he equsl to the duties. Ile had been a useful member of the Board and would hold the position with honour to himself as well as to themselves. (Applause). Mr Davies seconded and Mr Linaker suitably responded. The Chairman welcomed Mr Dean on his return after his long illness, and also wished Mr T Baxter a happy time on his holiday visit to America. Mr Morris also endorsed the chairman's remarks and both gentlemen suitably responded. A letter was read from the Clerk to the Swansea Man asking the beard to support the following resolutions. (a) "That the Local Government Board be asked to take such steps as to them seem necessary to prevent the disfranchisement of men not in any may the cause of the coal strike, but who have had to accept Poor Law relief. (13) That this Board urge upon Parliament to grant powers to all rating authorities throughout the country to impose and levy on the new valuation, being carried out under the finance Act of 1910, n tax on the value of land for local purposes. Mr Morris said he would support the first resolution. Under the great stres3 of the past month it was no fault of the men themselves but the fault of others that they had to seek aid. They ought not to be dis-

franchised simply through having received a few shillings from the unions ? Mr Hallows seconded and the first resolution was supported by the Board. In regard to the second resolution, Mr. Linaker (sea) asked if it was intended that the local governing bodies would have the powf r to impose t ixes on undeveloped land in addition to the Imperial taxation. The Clerk : I suppose he r(sdlution is meant to convey that. Mr Linaker : In that case it would be a gross injustice. He proposed that they should not support the resolution. Mr Morris seconded and the resolution was not entertained.

The following resolution was proposed by Mr Wood- ward, and Mr T Wright seconded --" That the Guar- dians hereby consent to the hiring of the premises known as Dutton Lodge, for a term not exceeding ten years, at RD annual rental not excetding £9O, the said premises to he appropriated as a home for children or for such other purpose relating to the relsef of the poor as the Guardians may with the consent of the Local Government Board direct." The resolution was carried unanimously.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL. ANNUAL MEETING. The annual meeting of the Newton District Council was held at the Town Hall. Ea rieseown, on Monday. On the motion of Mr C B F Borrou, seconded by Mr W Collingwood, Mr W Thompson was re-elected chairman. —Mr Collingweod moved the re-appointment of Mr L E Pilkington as vice-chairman.--Mr E L Bent seconded and the motion was unanimoualy carried.—The whole of the members of the Council were elected to the various committees, and a new committee was formed, called the Housing and Town Planning Gimrnittee.—A proposition by Dr Latham that the Press and public be admitted to the committee meetings was defeated by casting vote of the chairman, five voting for the motion and five against it. MR. J. A. SEDDON ON TRAGEDIES OF INDUSTRY.

Mr. J. A. Seddon, the Labour candidate for Newton Division, speaking on Tuesday night at a meeting near Binge Church, made a sympathetic reference to the Titanic 'calamity, and said they felt glad that even to-day, notwithstanding the deadening effect of industrial conditions, notwithstanding the grasping greed of commercialism that took no account of human life, th'y found that men had not lost that heroism and nobility of character that eemonstrated that human nature was not wholly bad, and he was convinced there was no man or woman who did not feel a thrill of joy when they thought of the noble way in which those men stood on the ill-fated ship knowing that they were going down to death. The miner. belonged to an industry where every year as many lives were lost as Wf re lost on the Titanic. The toll of labour is over 4 000 fatal accidents in a year in this country, aid it had been said over and over again that a large proportion of that great total of lives could be saved if in the mining and other dangerous industries proper regulations were enforced. But in the mad rush for profit, and to get rich quick, human life was of very little account, and the loss of life in the mines and on the high seas was taken little account of unless it was marked by some great ratastropheas that of the Titanic. In 1909 no less than 1,493 rulwrs %yore killed by ac dents at at their work, and that appalling loss of life had been going on year after yoar. Organised capital was the most brutal power on God's earth.

'est Lancashire Mission.

On Wednesday evening at the Atkinscu's Cafe the Rev WII Oraxton delivered an address setting forth the doctrine of the new church on "Miracles." An interest- ing conversation followed and many questions were asked. There was a somewhat larger attendance than the average, the local friends having assembled to bid good-bye to the lecturer, this being his final visit prior to his removal to London. Mr II Law on behalf of those present, made a very kind reference to the help he and others had received from the lectures and meet- ing n held since last October, and wished Mr Claxton every success in his new sphere of labour, and expressed the desire that he might some day visit Warrington again.

St. John's Presbyterian Church.

Preaching on Sunday evening from Jer. 22-16 on compassion to men, the Rev W Reid placed love of humanity beyond power, or genius, or even the morally correct life, and stated that it stood first tio a means of knowing God, outstripping every other avenue of know- ledge. All enthusiam for humanity however, even that. outside the Christian Church, has its fonrce and inspira- tion in the Spirit of Christ by which it exists, and by which the ideal of humanity is constantly being raised. This was evidenced by the "Titanic "disaster. • Man's genius might be defeated, and there came times when what he considers to be the greatest thing abont him— his scientific mind—is seen to give place to the primal passions (.f courage and love for others. Because W T Stead and others had put the Cross into their lives, and forgotton self in the interests of others, and were prepared to die that others might live, they were able to know God. Mrs Miller rendered the solo " Nearer my God to Thee "and the congregation rang " Eternal Fa. her," " Rescue the perishing," and "Rock of Agee."

Wicliffe P.S.A.

There was a rather small attendance on Sunday at the meeting of this society presided over by the :-ecretary. Mr. Jest,ph Owen gave the address upon " Brotherhood which meant a helping hand, a sympa- thetic look, and loving counsel and a strong feeling of love one to another. The hymns of the meeting had all special bearing upon the great sorrow, that everyone felt for those who were mourning the loss of dear ones through the "Titanic " disaster. Mr. Warburton sang " Tne sailor's grave," and " Lead kindly light." The hymn and prayer for those upon the seas were joined in. The Secretary as chairman said there were several things done in the moments of death that would live on. The lady who refused to be parted from her husband in death as they had lived together for more than 40 years. The captain who saved the little child and the band playing as they went down into the water "Nearer my God to Thee" had all proved themselves courageous and heroic. Mr. McEachern, and Mr. Healey, also took t.art in the meeting. The meeting feelingly joined in " Nearer by God to Thee." Primitive Methodist Church, Legh-street.

The Sunday school of the above church celebrated its anniversary on Sunday. The preacher morning and evening was Rev. Prof. A. L. Humphreys, M.A.B.D , one of the tutors at Hartley College. Manchester. In the morning the professor took for his text John ii. 7 to 10, and said he was constrained to preach on this occasion on the subject " Divine Providence." Many people believed that God ruled over all, but the difficulty arose when the personal application was placed before its, many were not satisfied because they did not hold what we might term a "guarantee form" that this Divine Providence worked for each person. He would like them to ask how does the guiding hand of God work ? Or is it possible for us to get out of the way or will of God and get into dangers ? God, said the preacher, does not generally work by miraculous medium but through ordinary channels. We must also remem- ber that God works through us and not to us. There- fore if there is defection in us, should we not be ready at the calling of God's voice, we did not receive the I message rightly and so we were out of the way and sometimes altered the very course of God. Christ heard the call to go again into Judea, and though His disciples reminded Him of the hostility of the Jew, yet, lie was assured it was the will of His Father because of His (Christ's) sense of duty and because it, was the will of God. God would watch over and the Divine Providence would be complete. The path of duty to Jesus was the path of safety and to every one who wished to know the will of God there should be made known that will, if they were sincere and walked in the day because there was light. It was possible for us to get out of the touch of the divine will and into the path of danger by human foolishness and if by this we brought some terrible thing to pass we ought not to blame Cod. In the great and teriible disaster which had happened so recently it would not be in accord- ance with the Divine Protectors will to speed through so dangerous a zone WS those great icebergs and the fog bound district would he to the passage of a vessel even so large as the Titanic. All seemed to be rush and a human desire to do things quickly, when at times we ought to bait, and wait upon God to know His will. We were out of tune with His will. We were not securely anchored and the atmosphere of rest was lacking. Our religion was short of devotion or faith. lie appeal. dto the congregation to eut their will in tune with that Higher Will and believe that God is all that He must be with those that obey. In the afternoon at 2.30, under the presidency of Mr C E Parker, of Penketh, a service was held. Mr Parker in his remarks gave some interesting anecdotes from his own family life and upon each one gave a lesson to the teachers and others engaged in the teaching of the children. Mesdames Hoyle and Cresswell were the leaders of an exercise given by thirteen scholars of the Sabbath school. The exercise was "Working for the Master" and the girls did credit to their leaders who had trained them, assisted by the superintendent (Mr B T Park es). At the evening mirvice the preacher gave a splendid f ermon based upon the subject" The Ideal City "(Rev. xxi. '2) and claimed that God desired and would have the help of man in the bringing forth of this city. The children and choir, conducted by Mr A Spruce, sang special tic /1108 at each service. Mr Banner was at the organ. The collections amounted to £I2 ss. 3d.

The annual two-days' sale of work in aid of the Trust Fund of the Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church was held in the lecture ball on Wednesday and Thursday. The arrangements had been in the hands of the ladies' row- ing meeting committee, and there was an inviting array of useful and fancy wares. In the ordinary working of their church there were expenses which must of necessity be met, but they were living in a neighbourhood which was of such a character that some of their friends were leaving and taking up their residenoe in more salubrious atmospheres. They were experiencing some difficulty in keeping up the ordinary contributions of the church to meet expenses. That sale bad been arranged sear by year in order that they might make up the deficiency in their annual returns. CHARITY ORGANISATION. ST. HELENS SOCIETI"S POSITION.

The annual meeiing of the St. Helens Charity Organ- isation Socisty was held on Tuesday at the Town Hall, Sir David Gamble, Bart., presiding. The annual report stated that during last year there was no slackness of work in St. Helens and the Labour Exchange had also had an influence in reducing the society's cases. The number of applications received was 229, of which 181 were relieved and 17 referred to the guardians. Alderman Martin said that the coal strike would make a difference in the next rep )rt, for the society had been kept busy. It might be that owing to changed circum- stances it would be necessary in the future to reconsider their position. The funds being exhausted, a special appeal was made for £5O.
