F. ByratY writes on betel( of the committee of Union of Carpente s and .luir.ers to complain that union traaesmen are prohibited from working on the mew Warrington Bridge; that the union rate of wages in these trades is not obterved by de contractors ; and that the Council is apathetic in the matter. THE CLOCKS.

MR. W. P. RYLANDS' REPLY TO MR. C. E. PARKER.

Sir,— I must tlAink Mr. Parker for his candid replies to my questions. They were in substance the only possible replies, and conceded the whole case for Tar;ff Reform which I personally c‘culd seek to

establish. Au nib reply to my fourth question Mr. Park er seems to have got unaccountably, at sea nod wandered irrelevantly into considerations of the export of metallic gold and the loan of capital abroad, alrich bad no bearing on the case. I suggested to Mr. Parker that the imported clicks could be paid for by the sale of for eign securities, i.e. by the absolute exportation and alienation of capital. and Mr. Parker agrees that 3:1,000 worth of imported clocks could be paid for by the exportation say of £l,OOO American railway lionds, and the capital of this country would be depleted to that extent, and in such a case therefore it would not be true that the importation of the clocks would necessitate the exportation of commodities affording work for British workingmen. 'I be fact that the American bonds in times g no by, and in c,nnection with an entirely differebt transaction were received in exchange for British goods, is irrelevant, and has no bearing cue way or the other upon the par ticular clock transaction. Mr. Parker still seems to have an idea that imports compel exports, and that this is done in some mysterious way by the operation of varying rates of excharge. If the balance o! imports and exports per cent, was merely the result of chance, the controlling adtion of exchange would inevitably be more apparent than it is, and while I agree exchange affords some controlling influence, an approximate balance of trade a natural result in itself. On another occasion I will endeavour to demonstrate this. For the present I must be content to express the opinion that broadly speaking an export compels payment in the form either of an import of commodities or the creation of foreign obligations due to this country. Unless, therefore, import duties reduced exports through increased cost of manufacture, the.effect on the aggregate imports would be nil. Commodities the import of which is restricted by import duties would be replaced by the import of comrnodities which it may be the policy of -the importing conntry to encourage. The foundation of the Free Trade argument enunciated by such writers as Adam Smith and Mill was that under Free Trade capital would flow natur- ally to those industries tor which a country was best suited, and would exhaust all outlets at home before being invested abroad. On that theory they conceived that with the na'ural protection afforded by geographical position, and the high cost of freight, even trades for which a country was only moderately suited would not lack capital, if sufficient capit ii were available. This theory was based upon conditions ruling prior to the days of limited liabiliry com- panies and ease of communication, and when there was an irresistible tendency for capitalists to exhaust all source of investment at home before facing the grave risk of launching into enterprise:across the seas. It was based upon the idea that geographical position and coat of transit must always give the home pro- ducer a great natural protection against foreign competition. Now in the days f cheap freight, ease of communication and limited liability, not only is all that natural advantage lost and any serious preference for home over foreign investments absolutely gone, but indeed a country like Germany actually employs a tariff as a means of fostering dumping, and as an assistance instead of a hindrance to her export trade. If therefore it is admitted that Britain is as well suited for the production of clocks as Germany, and that other, possibly more favourable, industries would not lack capital if any were ercouraged to flow to the clock industry, the whole Free Trade argument, as applied to that trade is gone. Mr. Parker admits it, and as I have said that admission establishes the case for Tariff Reform as I conceive it. Concede that it would be well for the country that any one industry, say clock making, might be pro- tected with advantage to the state, and it follows that a blind policy of Free Trade is not ideally perfect. If one industry, then probably others, the extent is only a matter of investigation and inquiry. If we as a eople are so stupid as not to he able to ascertain how far such a policy should go, I have nothing more to say, but I decline to think so badly of the intelligence of my fellow cour.try men. The policy of protection, as Mr. Parker admits, does nct check the exportarion of clocks by Germany, and in that country I have always understood high corn taxes, in the interest of a strong agrarian interest, have long obtained, and indeed have been a source of much controversy. To my mit.d the or:cation of urn forms no essential part of the policy of Tariff Reform so far as it relates to the protection of British industries from undesirable foreign competition, and of course no sane people would suggest the taxation of pure raw materials. The suggested import duty f2s per quarter upon foreign Corn, and ~ ' foreign corn only, has been put forward as a price worth paying to secure for British manufactures a Preference in the large and growing markets of our Colonies. Such a small tax upon a portion only, and a diminishing portion at that, of the corn imported into this country could hardly be appreciable, and I should not like to endorse an opinion that it would afford any serious assistance to liritish agriculture. In any event it is undeniably a part of the Tariff Reform programme to reduce the present food taxation, from which I believe a Customs revenue is raised larger than that of any other country in the world. For this reason I decline to attach any importance to the food tax question. If the advantage to be gained from reciprocal trading with the Colonies is not great enough to be wortO the small sacrifice inso lved, by all means let us have none of it. The whole point underlying the Tariff Reform position lies in the answer to the question whether there is any industry which might be pr tected with advantage. Mr. Parker's answers to my question involves him in acquiescence in the view that the country would benefit from Protection for the clock industry. That is sufficient. The problem then becomes one for experts, and must be considered and settled by experts with the facts before them. Amateur proposals fr a Tariff are worthless, and indeed impertinent, jest as a refusal to depart from the policy of free imports for no other reason than fear lest we lack sufficient intelligence to be trusted to do it wisely, is unphilosophical, and just as a refusal to appreciate that changed condition may require changed methods, resembles rather the habits of an ostrich—Yours faithfully, W. PETER RYLA.NDS.

April 23rd, 1012.

Town Hall Ward Election. Sir, -As I walked to tender my vote in the Town Hall Ward yesterday, I was struck with the number of conveyances with tlie blue label on them, and the great array of workers on the side of the tory candidate men of all grades from the Baronet and Lady Greenall (with the yellow carriage), and the sprinkling cf directors of the Tory newspaper. An array of publicans, and their wives representing the two Brewelies, also a number of hangers Cu from the titre( t corners as messengers. I asked myself what can stand against all this wealth and influence? Looking on the oppcsite side of the street, I saw a band of willing working men and ladies, about three one-horse conveyances but no representatives of wealth, or large rnanufacturets. On this side of the ttreet you have a quiet reliacce upon the intelligence of-thinking men, trusting to a wider outlook than Tariff Reform, the Tory plank in municipal contests '1 his election is the fol et unner of others soon to be contested in the town. I fear the introduction of politics into these contests. will be seen and felt in our Council Chamber, and may cause an amount of ill-fee'ing there which at present does not exist, but, which, if continued, will tesult in the election of the Mayor and Aldermen frcm a political stand-point and this will be a bad day for Warrington. —Yours etc., A VOTER.

GREAT FIRE AT WARRINGTON.

Messrs. Gartons Warehouse Gutted.-E60,000 Damage.

THE FIRE AT MESSRS. GARTONS' WAREHOUSE ON THURSDAY EVENING.

The large commercial warehouse of Messrs Gartons, Limited, the y orld fmous firm of seed I specialists, situated in At play Fields, Warri, gton,l was destroyed by fire on Thutsday evening. It is many years since such a dis3strous outbreak (courted in Warrington, the flames spreacing with such amaz- I• ing rapidity that in less than half an hour after the alarm was oiven, it was seen that the building was doomed. The damage is estimated at between i. 00,000 and .£70,000. It is covered by insurance. Shortly after six o'clock, when the workmen had finished for the day, a watchman discovered that the middle of the top storey of the warehouse was ablaze. The building, which was erected about two years and a half ago, was 180 feet long by GO feet wide and comprised six storeys and bisement. The iffices ,of the firm adjoin the warehouse but fortunately ale entirely separate from it At the west end of the warehouse was situated a structure u-ed as sto erooma and stables. The bufldirg which fell a victim to the flames was used as a commercial warehouse for the execution of orders and contained on each flier very valuable machinery, patented by the firms. Within a few minutes of the discovery of the out- break, the flames got beyond contr.d. Messtges were sent for the Corporation, Mess's. Crosfield's and the Wilderspool Fire Brigades which responded to the call with great promptitude. From the utset, how- ever, the task of the firemen was a hopeless one and their efforts were directed to preventing the destruc- tion of other property. A considerable time elapsed before anythicg like an. adequa.e volume if water could be obtained to play upon the wareh. use. The town main and a supply from the London and North-Western Railway Company's reservoir at Arpley Station were tapped. Unfortu- nately the Mersey, upon the town bank of which the warehouse was sitnatel, was at low tide, and practic- ally all the power of the larger engine cf the Corporation brigade was requi.ed to lift the water and conse- quently the jets could not he got to work at a very great height. The flaurs descended from floor to floor with appalling swiftness and in less than two hours the building was gutted. . .

There was soil). ethin—g awe-inspiring about the spectacle when the fire was at its height Tongues

of flame) and spalks shot upwards and could to seen for several miles round. Th-, huildin:z was like a I oarinq furnace and the !wet was te,rific. A dense miss of smoke also belched forth, iAdit4tivg to people i.t all parts of the to/rn th.t something unusual was happening. Thous.nda p opleptsbed to the sct-ne of the oi4aster tut a e,trocg force of police wig on duty tinder the control of tbe Chief Constable (Ir. M. Nicholls) and rod order was maintained.

About threequarters if an hour after the outbreak the rrof collapsed and as the debris tAisted thti white hot girders, the wall supports were weakened and bulged out of the perpendicular to an Alarming extent There was a great fa 1 of brick work from the top of the eat gable end several firemen who were working in the in:mediate vicinity had narrow escapes. Soon ::,ftsrwards the main wall on the side facing the Wairit.gton Cricket Ground began to buckle and large sections crashed to the ground at frequent :nterva's. Firemen were again in great per:l of injury and a member of the Corporation brigade—one of the brothers Blackshaw —had a miraculous escape. He was about to obtain some tackle from a reel cart which was standing close no ter the wall when several hundredweights of masonry were dislodged and fell upon the cart which Was splintered into matchsood. Fortunately Blackshaw succeeded in keeping clear of the debris. The Titanic and Lifeboats.

Sir,—The terrible disaster to the " Titantic " has bniught vividly before everyone the great danger which "those who down to the sea in ships" incur, and at the same time a feeling of gladness must prevail in the knowledge that round our rock-bound coasts the Royal National Lifeboat Ins.itution main tains a great fleet of 283 lifeboats. How well the efficiency of the Service is upheld is evidenced by the fact that over 50,000 lives have been saved, including (in 1892) 376 persons from the North German Lloyd as. Eider, and (in 1907) -150 from the White Star Liner Suevic, whilst only on 16th March last the Newhaven and Eastbourne lifeboat-men saved 83 from the P. and 0. Liner Oceans. Our Institution is suppoited entirely by Voluntary contributions.—l am, Sir, your obedient servant, ERNESI' MORRIS, Hon. Sec. (P.oyal National Lifeboat Institution. Warrington and District Branch). Can Britain Feed Herself?

Sir,—Where men are wont to meet, the above sub- ject is often diecuesed. Very many who have not seriou.dy studied the subject are under the delusion that this nation is unable to feed herself, and Conse- quently throw the subject ta the winds, with the result that they fail to notice the tree...tire and storehouse which lies at the nation's feet. Seeing that the fitcel subject is and will in the future be more to the front than it ever has been in the past, the f Bowing extract of recent date from a practical farmer who knows the subject may be of enlightenment to some of your readers: Total acreage of the 13ritish Tsles 5G million acres including excluding water, 32 millions cultivated, 21 million not cultivated. Of these take one fifth This gives us 11 million of acres to c...ma for wheat for every year. Allowing every acre to grow seven sacks of wheat and the seven Becks of wheat to make four sacks of flour, four by 11 are 44. This gives us 41 millions sscks of flour, a slck of flour for every individual in the British false tr 100 loaves of bread a year for every individual, or two 4h loaves for every individual a seek and a dumpling sometimes. All grown at hoint. Brea riatt3tl-8 instead of dread noughts. Production instead of destruction. Work for all. Food for all Poverty for no one. YCTERAN rAnmEn. An authoi ity corneutiog on this, says " Veteran farmer is right If nll our cultivable land were tilled on market gardening lines we could easily raise every ounce of wheat and meat required for our people. If we tilled it en Mr Vincent's lines we could become -cline mores wheat and meat exporting country. May I point out m reference to the above that 50 years ago, according to the" Daily News, "there were upwards of two minims engaged iii these Isles in agricultural pursuits —now only one million—and that according to the ratio of increase of population we ought now to have four millions employed. They admit the vital importance it is to the nation's exist- ence, but still fight for foreign markets, as against home markets and for worship at tbe shrine f the god of cheapness, which lea-is to the sacrifice of so much of our best blood and also overlooking the fact that we get now the bulk of our tiod requirements Lour Protected markets. What a strange anomaly ? Yet they bonder at for.d prices going up. May I also submit the following for cotsideration ? Would it not ba much better for the nation's true welfare if the workers of this country were employed in the op( n air surrounded with the winds of Heaven and undr r the rays of the glorious sun, which means life and vigor to the race, instead of being cooped up up in cotton factories and workshops breathing an artificial attn. sphere ? No wonder many of cur workers are old men at 50, in fact a large number even fail to reach that age. Fortunately many in the nation are waking up to

the fact that the ambition to be the workshop for the world is nothing leiqi than a false ideal aud makes for human tragfdy and that on the other band it is far better to build up a noble, healthy and sturdy race.— Yours truly, E. ENGLAND.

GENERAL BOOTH.

The vtteran General uf the Salvation Army is announced to siit this town, and as will be seen by the notice in this paper, he will give an address in the Hippodrome, in the afternoon and evening, of Sunday. At a time when most men have retired from active engagements, this fine old gentleman is busily engaged in inspecting his forces, laying plsns for Continental tours, and these tours, we are informed, are not for the purpose (.1 change, pleasure, and health, but for the one purpose of giving heart and eximple to his tnices, as well as prosecuting what is described es the business of his life, v,z., lifting up of the por and ' destitute men and women of this and o'her lands, and increasing the efficiency of his Army and Sucial workers.

We doubt not, but that large congregations will assemble to listen to this venerable man, who for nearly seventy yeats has been engaged in philan- thropic wot k, and who for nearly fifty years has been at the head of the movement which he founded, and which has now established itself ia nearly all the countlies of the eat th.

Upon inquitiel we learn tthat the General is still active and vigoicus in boy and spirit, and that the difficulty with his eyesight, which has been, a source of anxiety to himself and the organisation, is receiving special treatment. The General is about to have an operation to remove the diincllty fr:m the eye, which will then leave it sound and good. One of General Booti,'s at‘sociiites end helpers in the meetings at War, ington is Brigadier F. Aspinall, who has been for 11 years chaplain in the social work of the Army. Mr. Aspinall is a Warringt,nian "bred and burn," and a son of the late Mr. John Aspinall, who formerly held the licence of the " Crown and Sceptre." Brigadier Aspinall left Wan ington 29 years pg.., but he hopes to meet some old Warrington friends during the week-end meetings.

THE BRABAZON SOCIETY.

Annual Sale of Work.

The work of the Brabazon Society is still being actively et:riled on in Warrington, and the efforts of that body are responsible for cheering the hearts of many unfortunates who are unable to provide adequate provision for themselves. The object of the committee is to provide the inmates of theoUnion hospital with the materials whereby they can make goods which are disposed of at a sale held annually and the proceeds are devoted entirely for their benefit. This 3ear the sale was held in the Whnuarleigh Café on Thursday after- noon and the opening ceremony was perf.,rmed by Mrs Roscoe Brunter. On every band there was ample evidence of the good work the Society was doing in the shape of dainty and useful articles of needlework which were laid out in S tempting manner on the stalls in charge of Mrs Venn, Misses Farrant,Robertoon, Rushworth, Hodgkiuson, G Pierpoint,D Brown,Hutton, Piget, Appleton, Broadbent (secretor)), M Broadbent. and N Fairclough. Mr 0 A Rusinvoßm presided at the opening ceremony and called upon Miss Brocdtent to give her ennual report. In doing so she thanked all for their presence that day. She had a capital set of lady helpers and the work of the sceety was going en as usual. (Applause.) They were all eager to do their share and took a personal interest in tto inmates. They only employed those who wore not supposed to he able to do anything and all who could help in other ways were kept fully employed by the nurses. Many of the people they visited were destined to spend the remainder of their days within the hospital and the Brahazon work to them was their one intrrest in life. (Applause.) In view of the fact that in the workhouse hospital there was every conceivable temperament and a good many grades of society, their visits there were anything but dull and they were amply repaid for any efforts they made. The inmates were very grateful and appreciative and they turned out some capital work. (Applause). She then presented a balance sheet which showed that there was a balance in hand of 12s. sd. Mrs BRUNNER, ins few words, declared the sale open and in proposing a vote of thank., Mr Rushworth said the inmates of the workhouse hospital were only too pleased to receive instructions at the hands of the committee. That sale va, arranged so that they could get. a further supply of materials for the inmates and also to be able to give the patients monetary grants and help in a variety of ways. (Applause). Rev W BRACECAMP seconded, and the motion was carried with acclamation.

A brisk sale fo-lio—w-e7l-a;d the sum of £l7 was realised

A WOMAN'S GIFT.

Knowledge spells health, as well as power. There is hardly a woman who would not be better for a little fresh light on her own hodßy conditions. What woman does not sillier at times from the distressing backaches, sick-headache or extreme fatigue, in a way which is not shared by husband or brother? It h 35 been well said that if men had to suffer the same pains and weariness which afflict weak and patient women, these men would be in bed three or four days every month. But the woman knows this is part of her inhetitance. She suffers and save nothing about it, even to the doctor. The doctor could save her some of the pain : the knowledge which he could impart would mean health and relief. But a woman does not fly to the doctor until she is suffering more than she can bear, A woman who became famous for relieving such suffering was Mrs. I..3dia E. Pinkham, and a hook of her private advice and hints to women, which has saved thou,,ands of them from untold suffetings can be had by any woman by writing fQr it to 'l he Lady Manager, co.) Lydia E. Pinkham Medicine Co, 42a, Hampstead Road, London, N.W. The book is sent in a plain sealed envelope ; and from the same address can be had the great medical discovery which made Mrs. Pinkham famous, Mrs. Lydia E. Pinkham's Vegetable Compound. PHILOMATHIC SOCIETY.

ANNUAL DINNER.

Of all the functions held annually in Warrington the dinner arranged by the Philomathic Society is one of the mcst successful. Since the formation of the society in 1901 steady progress has been made and at thepresent time the activities of the institution are o ide- spread. The society has been fortunate in the past in securing many eminent men to lecture to them, and we understand there are many educational treats in store for the members in the future.

This year the annual dinner Wbl3 held in the Patten Arms Hotel on Wednebday evening The president, Mr- J. Ball, presided over a large and jovial company, amongst whom were : Mr E E Burgess (vice-president), Mr S E Barlow (hon. secretary), Mr D J Dunn (hon. treasurer), and the o' her officials of the society are, Messrs II H Taylor, J H Mason, J Walsh and H Sankey. There were alse preseut at the dinner Mr E Acton (Manchester), Canon Stevenson, Bev II Gray, Mr H G Robert., Mr S Butgr as, and Councillor Hawthorn. _

The menu was an excellent one and reflects great credit on tie caterers, indeed everything was of the best. There were many interesting after-dinner speeches made bo h in the proposition of the various toasts, and in the replies. 'The rapid progress which has been made in Warrington in many rtspects, provided good matter for tbe speeches when the toast of the County Borough was to be honoured. The propnser Mr Rathbone emphasised the usefulness of health and education, and tendered a suggestien to the effect that the children of the town should be educated for the particular trade and profession which they might choose as a means of earning a livelihood. Councillor Hawthorn replied to this toast in a very able speech in the c urse of which he said that the expense of educ ding the children should be borne by the Govern- ment ar d not Ly the local ratepayers. His contention w s that the childten were being educated for the benefit of the nation and therefore the expense should be borne nationally. Needless to say this proposition to relieve tits ratepayers was greeted with loud and long applause. The other toasts were equally well disposed of and the company thoroughly enjoyed the witticisms wh.ch Mr. Acton expressed in replying to the toast of" Our Guests." It was rather unfortunate that the speeches towards the finish had to be merci- lessly curtailed owing to lack of time and the toast of "the President" had to be dispensed with. The toast of" The King "having been proposed by the PIISSIDENT and honoured by the company. Mr. A. H. itaTfinoNe in an interesting speech gave " The County Borough of Warrington. lie had a feeling of pride in his native town. There were more important towns than Warrington ; more magnificent and beautiful towns—(" no, ")—but there was none so dear to the hearts of Warringtonians as Warrington Itself. (hear, hear.) It had had citizens and traditions to he proud of and the town still beide position of some little importance in the commercial world. "We are rather apt cont;nued the speaker to interpret a toast of this kind in a somewhat narrow sense particularlythose who are engaged in commercial pursuits. On analysing the causes which led to the prosparity of the town it seems to me that commerce is wedged between, on the one hand, health and education and on the other, art and drama. That is to say the prosperity of commerce &pearls to a certain extent on the progress of health and education, and art and drama on the other hand depend on the prosperity of commerce. (Hear, hear.) So far as health is concerned no-one can dany that wise steps are being taken by the Mayor and Corpora- tion and the officials of the Corporation connected with that department, towards the improvement of the health conditions for the benefit of both old and young. There hes undoubtedly been an improvement in the standard of health of the borough and that improvement wi'l be fully maintained on the lines followed by the gemlemen I have named." It was undoubtedly a wise and proper course to condemn property which was not fit for human habition and to insist on better housing coaditions—(hear, hear)—and also it must be the right course to see that the younger generation was strengthened physically by proper food and judicious instruction and to foster a greeter pride in the parents of the youngsters alto came to swell our population. When the education period came along the teachers would find their efforts more readily responded to and the teaching they were there to give more readily assimilated. (Hear haar.) Much had been done but much remained to be done. The facilities for education were immeasurably greater than ever before. His suggestion to the authorities, though it might not be new was that they should specialise and (Attests scholars for the particular trades or professions in which they showed a natural interest and aptitude and in which they would find good employment By this means and the increased facilities in education our commercial prosperity would be maintained. Most of our local trades were having a brisk time and these conditions would continue for some time. Unemploy- ment was never at a lower ebb than at present. (Applause.) In regard to art and drams, even there Warrington war well represented. They claimed bir Luke Fades as one of their citizens who received his early training in their midst. Then there was Alderman Arthur Bennett, who though he might not be an art'st painting in oils, had painted the good old town in print and had produced a picture of War- rington whi.:ll B °ugh it might be regarded as idealis.io would no doubt lesd to a proper realism. Time worked wonders as they were shown by the examples at the bottom of Bridge-street and at Howley. (Laughter.) In the realm of drama he thought Mr. Harold Smith would not, object, as Borough Member, to be styled a Warringtonian and those who bad seen him aaould agree he was not an unworthy rapresentative in that direction. (Applause)' Conncillor HAIVTIIORST, in response, said he was deputising for the Mayor who should have responded to the toast ht who,o,ving to unforseen circumstances, was unavoidab'y absent. Having referred to the origin of the title of "County Borough," the speaker pointed out that from the moment the town altained that dignity, the population increased, until at the present time it exceeded the 70,000 mark. They recognized the great giver of that in the motto on their coat of arms " Deus Dat Incrementurn," " God giveth the increase ' and he thought Canon Stevenson was right when he mentioned that that alluded to the rates. (Laughter). lie thought, however, that they were giving value for the money expended ("queation")—for practically the whole of the increase had been involved in education, lie believed that if they examined ordinary local rates prior to the time of the Education Act, they would find that they were somewhere near the present amount. The addition had been compelled y the Education Committee. They, as Councillors were TROUBLED WITH Tllll INIQUITIES OF TIIE EDUCATION CONIMITIEN.

lie thought the question of education should be a national one and that the total expenditure should come from the Government and not from the local rates. (Hear, hear). Warrington was at a disadvantage for they bad important bridge schemes connecting Lanca- shire and Cheshire and wide good roads leading through the town, but the Roads Board which was recently formed had yet to see the necesiiity of giving a town like Warrington a grant. The whole of the grant had gone to the County Councils,and Warrington had been left without a single coot. to carry en that important work. When the Commissioners met they might think that they had a claim upon them, and that they were entitled to something in that respect. Shortly they were to hold a health week. Addresses on health aubjecte would be delivered and the object was to give the people certain instruction on health topics. The difficulty, however would be to get hold of the right people to whom such instructions, would be valuable. Another point was that if they had a health week once a year why not have one every week in the year? The educa- tional side of the health week, however, would he of come value to Warrington and he hoped they would not let it pass unnoticed. At the pm sent time the death- rate in vVarrington was much lower than in the Manchester and I icerpool districts. That was son e- thing to congratulate themselves upon. Steps had been taken by the heslth committee in regard to insanitary property; trained visitors were going to the homes of the people and they had es'ablished a sanatorium at Sankey for the treatment of consumptives. (Applause.) They were hoping that such measures would help them to still further reduce the death rate. Much remained to be done. The legacits of the psst, although some of them were rich in historical associai ions and antiquity, were mixed blessings and involved the expenditure of large sums of money in removing some of the noisome courts and passages. There was also expenditure involved in the construction of wider blidg a and thoroughfares and they asked for the ratep(vera' forbearance and also for their goodwill when the rates showed a tuidency to move upward. (Applause). Mr. A. S. ELLIOTT in proposing the toast of" Our Guests" said they recognised them is kindred spirits and they had invited them to share their board because they were lovers of learning along with them. In regard to names the assumption of they oft-times implied two limitations. First cf all tlvire was the sense of limita- tion in the individual himself, expresaing itself after, the manner that if a man was a Philomath y name then he could not possibly be a Lit. and Poilosoph in spirit. (Laughter.) The other limitation seemed to resolve itself into a feeling that all the activities and devolopments of exteric persons was limited until those poor people could assume the recognised designation. Oh dear ! this Talismanic, narre ! We were living in a dire age of dominating dernminations. A man now-a- days tacked labels upon himself by the dozen and fondly imagined he was those things, failing to recog- nise that no mere name would endow a man but that every man might endow a name. Thus it was found that the wisest men in any age were the ones with the fewest labels and those were the men who deserved them most. Let them wear their nines humbly and nobly that others, perhaps, seeing the spirit they evinced and not knowing their names, might be by that means able to guess them. (Applause). The choir under the direction of Mr Frank Hickman, the organist, gave an expressive rendering of the anthem " Crossing the bar" (Sir F Bridge) and at the dig" of the service they sang Gounod's meted molar° " Gallia," the poles being sympathetically kterpreted by Mrs Arbor Booth. Cairo-street Chapel. , In the course of the service at Cairo-street Chapel on Sunday morning, the Rev. Dr. Stanley Mellor made the fol'owing reference to the bra of the Titanic feel that I ought not to let this day of our common' worship pass without some reference to the vast and 'awe-inspiring tragedy that has clouded a world in horror and in grief. Yet there is nothing to be said ta the last and ultimate question of the why and the wherefore which l”s been wrung with tears from so many breaking hearts, and which in the end man's soul will ale ays ask of God, there seems no clear answer from the impenetrable silence. But who shall say that the light of eternal things was not there also, brighter than the at rs that sa coldly viewed the awful scene? There was heroism, self-dsnial, devotion to duty, and the high, adventurous spirit of man that will not accept even death as a defeat ; and where these things are, who shall say thet there is not also God Our sympathy gors to those oe whom the burden of the tragedy tas fallen most heavily, yet the tears we shed in secret are trot for them only, but for the whole mystery of life and for the immitigable doom of a so glorious humanity that conquers even in death.

[BY REDCAP]. rAVEROLLES. Faverolles are of a French origin. They are an extremely useful variety Gotd layers and excellent table birds. As a pure breed they are, I think, abc,ut the best general purplse fowl we have. The pullets commence to lay early, and the cockerels (being quick growers) are soon ready for market purposes. They are sitters, but do not spend as much of their time in this manner as acme o her varieties do. The Faverolles are also of a very quiet nature ; aft. of netting is ample to keep them in bounds, therefore they are suitable as a suburban man's foil. It is very annoying when one's fowls are constantly getting over into neighbour's prem'ses.

SHAKESPEARE FESTIVAL AT STRATFORD. The three weeks' festival to commemorate the birth- day of Shakespeare was opened on Monday at Stratford-on-Avon. The ancient borough is en fete, a large number of visitors having arrived, including many distinguished representatives of foreign countries. Shakespeare's plays are being produced by the Benson Company during the festival. Sir Sidney Lee on Monday opened sn interesting exhibition of Shakespearean heraldry and antiquities.

the crowd, whose original purpose in assembling had not been to endeavour to dissuade, peacefully or other- wise, men from working, or of destroying the property of the colliery company. There were at the collieries two large accumulations known as dirt heaps—material that bad come out of the mines with the coal, and temporarily discarded as unsaleable for the time being. Up and down the country masters had generously allowed the wives and children of workmen to get what salvage they could from this sort of heap, but this particular company had declined to do that, and there had been threatenings and high words and no doubt these people gathered together to see what was going on. Counsel suggested, in view of tie excellent characters hitherto ofathe prisoners, that the case might be met by binding them over. His lordship said that the way he bad decided to deal with the two prisoners must not be considered as justifying What they had pleaded guilty to. It was a serious offence, and it must be borne in mind that people could not do this kind of thing with impunity. No good purpose was ever served by that sort of violence, and the time would come, unless people took due warning, when severer sentences would hlve to be passed for these offences. In view of prisoners' good characters and the fact that they were sincerely penitent for what they bad done, be would take the responsi- bility of binding them over.

LE ZOUTE-KNOCKE -NEAR OSTEND— The recent weather has been splendid for the golf course, which is in fine condition. It is said by those who should know, to be one of the best 18 hole courses on the Continent, and has that additional charm for all lovers of the health-giving game of being close to the shore. where there is a beautiful beach of fine white sand some fifty yard wide at low tide, and extending about twenty-five miles along the coast, running by Ostend and Blankenberghe and along to the frontier of Holland, only a short distance from Le Zoute-Knocke. Owing to the fine beach and good bathing, this golfing station is a great favourite with the children.
